# Curved Kakeya problems and the projective geometry of paths

**arXiv ID**: 2607.27629v1
**Authors**: Shaoming Guo, Larry Guth, Arian Nadjimzadah, Minxing Shen, Ruixiang Zhang
**Published**: 2026-07-30
**Categories**: math.CA, math.DG
**Comments**: 90 pages, 2 figures. Comments welcome!
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2607.27629v1

## Abstract

We introduce a general framework for curved Kakeya problems in $\mathbb{R}^n$, encompassing those arising from Hörmander-type oscillatory integrals. Every family of curves determines a spray geometry, which allows us to use the projective geometry of paths in the study of curved Kakeya problems. We focus on the two extremes of the "best" and "worst" possible behaviors of curved Kakeya sets.   We characterize when the incidence structure underlying Wolff's hairbrush argument persists. In particular, we prove that the existence of many totally geodesic surfaces, as required by Wolff's hairbrush argument, is equivalent to projective flatness of the associated spray. Within this projectively flat class, Bourgain's condition provides a clean dichotomy: when it holds, the family is direction-equivalent to a Bochner--Riesz type family of lines and satisfies the Katz--Wolff condition, and thus the Wang--Zahl result is applicable; when it fails, every totally geodesic surface supports a two-dimensional Kakeya set.   We also show that under an extra semi-algebraic assumption, a family of curves in $\mathbb{R}^3$ admits a curved Kakeya set of Hausdorff dimension $2$ if and only if it admits a curved Kakeya set contained in a surface. Equivalently, if this compression is absent, every associated curved Kakeya set has dimension strictly greater than $2$.

## Curved Kakeya problems and
the projective geometry of pathsShaoming Guo, Larry Guth, Arian Nadjimzadah,
Minxing Shen and Ruixiang Zhang
With an appendix written by Terence Tao

## Abstract

We introduce a general framework for curved Kakeya problems inℝn\mathbb{R}^{n}, encompassing those arising from Hörmander-type oscillatory integrals. Every family of curves determines a spray geometry, which allows us to use the projective geometry of paths in the study of curved Kakeya problems. We focus on the two extremes of the “best” and “worst” possible behaviors of curved Kakeya sets.

We characterize when the incidence structure underlying Wolff’s hairbrush argument persists. In particular,
we prove that the existence of many totally geodesic surfaces, as required by Wolff’s hairbrush argument, is equivalent to projective flatness of the associated spray. Within this projectively flat class, Bourgain’s condition provides a clean dichotomy: when it holds, the family is direction-equivalent to a Bochner–Riesz type family of lines and satisfies the Katz–Wolff condition, and thus the Wang–Zahl result is applicable; when it fails, every totally geodesic surface supports a two-dimensional Kakeya set.

We also show that under an extra semi-algebraic assumption, a family of curves inℝ3\mathbb{R}^{3}admits a curved Kakeya set of Hausdorff dimension22if and only if it admits a curved Kakeya set contained in a surface. Equivalently if this compression is absent, every associated curved Kakeya set has dimension strictly greater than22.

## 1Introduction

## 1.1Informal introduction

We begin with an informal discussion of the main results, postponing precise definitions until later. A curved Kakeya problem starts with a2​(n−1)2(n-1)-parameter family of curve segmentsℓξ,w\ell_{\xi,w}inℝn\mathbb{R}^{n}, whereξ∈ℝn−1\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}records the “direction” andw∈ℝn−1w\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}records the “position”. These curves satisfy a non-degeneracy condition so that locally each point and each tangent direction determine a unique curve. The corresponding curved Kakeya set contains a curveℓξ,w​(ξ)\ell_{\xi,w(\xi)}for each directionξ\xi. The key model case is when the curvesℓξ,w\ell_{\xi,w}are (segments of) the lines(w,0)+ℝ​(ξ,1)(w,0)+\mathbb{R}(\xi,1), and the corresponding curved Kakeya set is a classical Kakeya set.

Overview of curved Kakeya sets:

Inℝ2\mathbb{R}^{2}, curved Kakeya sets are known to have full Hausdorff dimension 2. It was originally discovered by Bourgain[BOU91], in the more special case of curves arising from Hörmander’s oscillatory integral problem, that the situation inℝ3\mathbb{R}^{3}is significantly more subtle, and finer geometric properties of the family{ℓξ,w}\{\ell_{\xi,w}\}plays a major role. There can even be curved Kakeya sets of Hausdorff dimension 2, and the known examples are contained in a surface.

Curved Kakeya maximal function estimates tend to fail generically too, though usually not as severely as having dimension 2. The still–mysteriousBourgain’s conditiondescribes the class that is not covered by the generic counterexample construction. It is conjectured that this condition is necessary and sufficient for optimal curved Kakeya maximal function estimates[NAD26,GWZ24].

Two motivating questions:

This paper arose out of the following two fundamental question about Kakeya sets of curves, roughly concerning their best possible and worst possible behaviors.

## Motivating Question 1.1(Best-case behavior).

Can the techniques that led to the resolution of the Kakeya set conjecture inℝ3\mathbb{R}^{3}be applied to Kakeya sets of curves? As a first step, what about Wolff’s hairbrush argument inℝ3\mathbb{R}^{3}? For which families of curves can such arguments go through?

## Motivating Question 1.2(Worst-case behavior).

When can a family of curves inℝ3\mathbb{R}^{3}admit a two-dimensional curved Kakeya set? Is compression of a full two-dimensional family of directions into a surface the only mechanism by which this can occur?

The geometric input to Wolff’s hairbrush argument:

Let us explore these questions starting with Question1.1. Wolff’s hairbrush argument shows that Kakeya sets inℝ3\mathbb{R}^{3}have Hausdorff dimension at least5/25/2[WOL95]. In the argument, one essentially reduces to studying a hairbrush: a set of lines passing through a fixed stem. There are two important ingredients needed to make the argument work.

First, the lines must organize into essentially disjoint planes around the stem—this does not require one to distinguish the direction parameterξ\xi. Another way to say this is that for each pair of intersecting curves, the set of curves intersecting the first two are contained in a common surface. For generic curves one would expect a volume instead of a surface. We call this thetotally geodesiccondition and it is formalized in Definitions1.11,1.21.

Second, there can’t be too many lines inside each plane (this is known as the Wolff axioms)—the separation of the direction parametersξ\xiguarantees that this holds. Nets Katz had another way of running the hairbrush argument which appeared in Wisewell’s work[WIS05]: for each pair of intersecting curves, the set of direction parameters corresponding to the curves intersecting the first two is a curve. We call this theKatz-Wolffcondition and it is formalized in Definitions1.10,1.20. For a generic family, one would expect these direction parameters to fill a two-dimensional region rather than a curve.

We should also note here that the resolution of the Kakeya conjecture of Wang–Zahl makes crucial use of the fact that tubes organize into rectangular prisms, which is closely connected to the totally geodesic condition.
Bourgain’s condition unfortunately does not guarantee the Katz-Wolff condition or even the totally geodesic condition, in light of an example from[NAD26].

Applying methods from projective spray geometry:

To understand how the conditions above interact, what they reduce to, and to provide a foundation for a more systematic study of curved Kakeya problems, we establish a correspondence between curve families{ℓξ,w}\{\ell_{\xi,w}\}and projective spray spaces. More precisely, after forgetting the distinguished direction labels, a family{ℓξ,v}\{\ell_{\xi,v}\}forms a local system of paths and hence arises as the family of unparameterized geodesics of an associated spray; this is our coarsest level of geometry. The curved Kakeya problem contains additional data recording which curves carry each direction parameterξ\xi; this distinction will be essential below. Proposition1.26makes this correspondence precise. It allows us to use projective invariants of sprays, including the Douglas and Weyl curvatures, to detect when the underlying path geometry is locally projectively flat. Our first main theorem uses this connection to relate the conditions above, and suggests that without a serious new idea, the answer to the last question in Question1.1is only classical Kakeya lines.

## Informal Theorem 1.3(Formally Theorem1.33).

Let{ℓξ,w}\{\ell_{\xi,w}\}be a non-degenerate family of curves inℝ3\mathbb{R}^{3}.
Thentotally geodesic⟺after a local diffeomorphism ofℝ3,the curves become straight lines;\displaystyle\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\begin{subarray}{c}\text{\rm after a local diffeomorphism of $\mathbb{R}^{3}$, }\\
\text{\rm the curves become straight lines};\end{subarray}totally geodesic+Bourgain’s condition\displaystyle\text{\rm totally geodesic}\;+\;\text{\rm Bourgain's condition}⟺Katz–Wolff\displaystyle\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\text{\rm Katz--Wolff}⟺direction-equivalent toa Bochner–Riesz-type familyℓξ,w​(t)=(ξ,ϕ​(ξ))−t​(w,1);\displaystyle\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\begin{subarray}{c}\text{\rm direction-equivalent to}\\
\text{\rm a Bochner--Riesz-type family}\\[-1.00006pt]
\ell_{\xi,w}(t)=(\xi,\phi(\xi))-t(w,1);\end{subarray}totally geodesic+¬Bourgain’s condition\displaystyle\text{\rm totally geodesic}\;+\;\neg\,\text{\rm Bourgain's condition}⟺extra-worst compression⏟as in Bourgain’s example (1.9)\displaystyle\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\underbrace{\text{\rm extra-worst compression}}_{\text{\rm as in Bourgain's example \eqref{250801e1_8}}}⟺defevery totally geodesic surface supportsa two-dimensional curved Kakeya set.\displaystyle\quad\overset{\rm def}{\Longleftrightarrow}\quad\begin{subarray}{c}\text{\rm every totally geodesic surface supports}\\
\text{\rm a two-dimensional curved Kakeya set.}\end{subarray}

In particular, within the totally geodesic—equivalently, projectively
flat—class, Bourgain’s condition distinguishes two opposite possibilities:
the Katz–Wolff/Bochner–Riesz regime and the extra-worst-compression regime.

We now turn to Question1.2, which we resolve in the semi-algebraic case as our second main theorem. The proof uses tools from semi-algebraic geometry.

## Informal Theorem 1.4(Formally Theorem1.34).

Let{ℓξ,w}\{\ell_{\xi,w}\}be a non-degenerate family of curves inℝ3\mathbb{R}^{3}, and furthermore the curves have a parameterization(ξ,w,t)↦ℓξ,w​(t)(\xi,w,t)\mapsto\ell_{\xi,w}(t)which is semi-algebraic. Then:The family admits a curved Kakeya set of Hausdorff dimension2.⇕The family admits a curved Kakeya set contained in a two-dimensional surface.\begin{array}[]{c}\text{The family admits a curved Kakeya set of Hausdorff dimension $2$.}\\[5.0pt]
\Updownarrow\\[5.0pt]
\text{The family admits a curved Kakeya set contained in a two-dimensional surface.}\end{array}

Moreover, if these conditions fail, then there existsκ>0\kappa>0, depending only on the family, such that every associated curved Kakeya setKKsatisfiesdimH​K≥2+κ.\text{dim}_{\mathrm{H}}K\geq 2+\kappa.

In this paper, we also include an appendix by Terence Tao which makes progress on understanding Bourgain’s condition. The goal is to provide a coordinate-invariant definition that uses natural objects in differential geometry, much in the spirit of our introduction of spray geometry. This definition gives a convenient way to generate all phase functions satisfying Bourgain’s condition, including some interesting examples failing the Katz-Wolff condition.

## 1.2Hörmander’s problems and related Kakeya problems: A brief review

In[HÖR73], Hörmander suggested to study the following oscillatory integral operatorsTN(ϕ)​f​(x,t):=∫ℝn−1ei​N​ϕ​(x,t;y)​a​(x,t;y)​f​(y)​dy,T^{(\phi)}_{N}f(x,t):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}e^{iN\phi(x,t;y)}a(x,t;y)f(y)\mathrm{d}y,(1.1)

which are often referred to asHörmander-typeoscillatory integral operators, orHörmander’s operatorsfor simplicity.
Herex∈ℝn−1,t∈ℝ,y∈ℝn−1x\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1},t\in\mathbb{R},y\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}(1.2)

andN∈ℝN\in\mathbb{R}is a large real number. To simplify notation, we often write𝐱=(x,t){\bf x}=(x,t). Moreover,ϕ​(x,t;y)\phi(x,t;y)is a smooth function, anda​(x,t;y)a(x,t;y)is a smooth function supported in a bounded open neighborhood of the origin. The bounds that we are interested in proving are of the form‖TN(ϕ)​f‖Lp​(ℝn)≲ϕ,a,p,ϵN−np+ϵ​‖f‖Lp​(ℝn−1),\|T^{(\phi)}_{N}f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{\phi,a,p,\epsilon}N^{-\frac{n}{p}+\epsilon}\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})},(1.3)

for everyϵ>0\epsilon>0and everyN≥1N\geq 1, and for a range ofppthat is as large as possible. Here the implicit constant is allowed to depend onϕ,a,p\phi,a,pandϵ.\epsilon.


If we takeϕ​(x,t;y)=⟨x,y⟩+t​|y|2,\phi(x,t;y)=\langle x,y\rangle+t|y|^{2},(1.4)

then we obtain the classical Fourier extension operator.


Fourier restriction conjecture.Letn≥3n\geq 3. Letϕ\phibe given by (1.4). The estimate (1.3) holds for everyp≥2​nn−1.p\geq\frac{2n}{n-1}.(1.5)

In[HÖR73], Hörmander asked whether the estimate (1.3) still holds for the same range (1.5) whenϕ\phiis a “small” perturbation of (1.4). To make this precise, Hörmander introduced the following non-degenerate condition.

## Definition 1.5(Hörmander’s condition for oscillatory integrals,[HÖR73]).

We say thatϕ\phisatisfiesHörmander’s conditionif on the support ofa​(x,t;y)a(x,t;y), it holds that
- (H1)

thendet​∇y2⟨∇𝐱ϕ​(𝐱;y),G0​(𝐱;y0)⟩|y=y0≠0.\left.\operatorname{det}\nabla_{y}^{2}\left\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\phi(\mathbf{x};y),G_{0}\left(\mathbf{x};y_{0}\right)\right\rangle\right|_{y=y_{0}}\neq 0.(1.7)

Hörmander[HÖR73]and Bourgain[BOU91]observed that when studying Hörmander’s problem for phase functionsϕ\phisatisfying Definition1.5, we can always apply elementary changes of coordinates so that phase functions can be written in the formϕ​(𝐱;y)=x⋅y+t​⟨y,A​y⟩+O​(|t|​|y|3+|𝐱|2​|y|2),\phi(\mathbf{x};y)=x\cdot y+t\langle y,Ay\rangle+O\left(|t||y|^{3}+|\mathbf{x}|^{2}|y|^{2}\right),(1.8)

In this sense, we say that (1.8) is a small perturbation of the phase function (1.4) for the Fourier extension operator. The form (1.8) is called anormal formforϕ\phiat the origin. If the matrixAAis positive-definite, we say that the phase functionϕ\phiiselliptic.


In[HÖR73], Hörmander asked whether for every phase functionϕ\phisatisfying Hörmander’s condition in Definition1.5, the estimate

Let us very briefly discuss the history of Hörmander’s problem, and we refer to[GHI19],[HI22],[GWZ24],[DGG+24],[CGG+25]and[NAD25]for more detailed discussions.

We restrict our discussion to the casen=3n=3, which is already interesting enough. Stein[STE86]proved that (1.3) holds for every phase function satisfying Hörmander’s condition in Definition1.5and everyp≥4p\geq 4if we taken=3n=3.
In[BOU91], Bourgain gave a negative answer to Hörmander’s problem by showing that if we taken=3n=3andϕ​(𝐱;y)=x⋅y+t​y1​y2+12​t2​y12,\phi({\bf x};y)=x\cdot y+ty_{1}y_{2}+\frac{1}{2}t^{2}y_{1}^{2},(1.9)

wherey=(y1,y2)y=(y_{1},y_{2}), then the estimate (1.3) cannot hold for anyp<4p<4. Indeed, regarding negative results, Bourgain[BOU91]proved much more. In order to state Bourgain’s result, let us first introduce the notion of Bourgain’s condition.

## Definition 1.6(Bourgain’s condition,[BOU91],[GWZ24]).

Letϕ\phibe a phase function satisfying Hörmander’s conditions. We say that it satisfies Bourgain’s condition at(𝐱0;y0)({\bf x}_{0};y_{0})if((G0⋅∇𝐱)2​∇y2ϕ)​(𝐱0;y0)​is a multiple of​((G0⋅∇𝐱)​∇y2ϕ)​(𝐱0;y0)​.\left(\left(G_{0}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\right)^{2}\nabla_{y}^{2}\phi\right)\left(\mathbf{x}_{0};y_{0}\right)\text{ is a multiple of }\left(\left(G_{0}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\right)\nabla_{y}^{2}\phi\right)\left(\mathbf{x}_{0};y_{0}\right)\text{. }(1.10)

The constant here is allowed to depend on𝐱0{\bf x}_{0}andy0y_{0}.

Bourgain[BOU91]proved that in the casen=3n=3, if the phase functionϕ\phifails Bourgain’s condition in Definition1.6at least at one point, then the estimate (1.3) already cannot hold for anyp<3+139.p<3+\frac{1}{39}.(1.11)

Such a result was generalized to a wider range than (1.11) and to everyn≥3n\geq 3in[GWZ24]. Moreover, in[GWZ24]it is proven that if Bourgain’s condition in Definition1.6holds at every point, then the estimate (1.3) holds for a wide range ofpp. We refer to[GWZ24]for the precise statement.


So far we have finished our discussion on Hörmander’s problem, and next we will discuss Kakeya problems associated to Hörmander’s operators.

## 1.3Finer characterizations for Hörmander-type oscillatory integrals: New concepts

To motivate our new conditions for Hörmander-type phases, let us think about the following question. Previously we have seen that Bourgain’s condition in Definition1.6is a necessary condition for theϕ\phi-Kakeya maximal estimate (1.15) to hold for the widest possible range1≤p≤n1\leq p\leq n. Is it possible that Bourgain’s condition is also sufficient?

If we run the polynomial partitioning algorithm (for instance repeat the argument in[HRZ22]) by combining the polynomial Wolff axiom proven in[GWZ24]under Bourgain’s condition, then in the casen=3n=3we obtain that (1.15) holds forp=7/3p=7/3and as a consequence we obtain that everyϕ\phi-Kakeya has Hausdorff dimension at least7/37/3under the assumption thatϕ\phisatisfies Bourgain’s condition.

However, Wolff[WOL95]already proved that the Hausdorff dimension of the standard Kakeya set is at least5/25/2, by using his hairbrush argument. It is therefore very natural to ask whether one can run Wolff’s hairbrush argument under Bourgain’s condition. The following notion seems to be the minimal assumption one needs to run the hairbrush argument111Here we run the version of the hairbrush argument by Katz, see for instance[WIS03], and we will see that it is strictly stronger than Bourgain’s condition, see Theorem1.33below.

## Definition 1.10(Katz-Wolff condition for Hörmander phases).

Letϕ​(𝐱;y)\phi({\bf x};y)be a phase function satisfying Hörmander’s condition.
Fory1,y2∈𝔹ϵϕn−1,y1≠y2y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathbb{B}^{n-1}_{\epsilon_{\phi}},y_{1}\neq y_{2},𝐱0=(x0,t0)∈𝔹ϵϕn{\bf x}_{0}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathbb{B}^{n}_{\epsilon_{\phi}}, andt1,t2≠t0t_{1},t_{2}\neq t_{0}, letxi∈ℝn−1x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}be the unique point such that(xi,ti)∈Γyi(ϕ)​(𝐱0),i=1,2.(x_{i},t_{i})\in\Gamma^{(\phi)}_{y_{i}}({\bf x}_{0}),\ i=1,2.(1.16)

Moreover, letw​(t1,t2)∈𝔹ϵϕn−1w(t_{1},t_{2})\in\mathbb{B}^{n-1}_{\epsilon_{\phi}}andy​(t1,t2)∈𝔹ϵϕn−1y(t_{1},t_{2})\in\mathbb{B}^{n-1}_{\epsilon_{\phi}}be the unique (if exist) solutions satisfying that the characteristic curveΓy​(t1,t2)(ϕ)​(w​(t1,t2))\Gamma^{(\phi)}_{y(t_{1},t_{2})}(w(t_{1},t_{2}))(1.17)

passes through the two points(x1,t1)(x_{1},t_{1})and(x2,t2)(x_{2},t_{2}). We say thatϕ\phisatisfies the Katz-Wolff condition ifrank​[∂t1y​(t1,t2),∂t2y​(t1,t2)]<2,∀t1,t2.\mathrm{rank}[\partial_{t_{1}}y(t_{1},t_{2}),\partial_{t_{2}}y(t_{1},t_{2})]<2,\ \ \forall t_{1},t_{2}.(1.18)

Definition1.10requires that for two intersecting curvesΓy1​(𝐱0)\Gamma_{y_{1}}({\bf x}_{0})andΓy2​(𝐱0)\Gamma_{y_{2}}({\bf x}_{0}), the direction set for the characteristic curves intersecting both of these two curves, given by{y​(t1,t2):t1,t2≠t0},\{y(t_{1},t_{2}):t_{1},t_{2}\neq t_{0}\},(1.19)

is one-dimensional.

## Definition 1.11(Totally geodesic conditions for Hörmander phases).

Under the same notation as in Definition1.10, we say thatϕ\phisatisfies the totally geodesic condition if for all choices of𝐱0{\bf x}_{0}andy1≠y2y_{1}\neq y_{2}, the union of curves⋃t1,t2Γy​(t1,t2)(ϕ)​(w​(t1,t2))\bigcup_{t_{1},t_{2}}\Gamma^{(\phi)}_{y(t_{1},t_{2})}(w(t_{1},t_{2}))(1.20)

forms a two-dimensional surface.

Definition1.11requires that for two intersecting curvesΓy1​(𝐱0)\Gamma_{y_{1}}({\bf x}_{0})andΓy2​(𝐱0)\Gamma_{y_{2}}({\bf x}_{0}), all the characteristic curves intersecting these two curves form a two-dimensional surface.


Wolff’s original hairbrush argument[WOL95]requires Definition1.11and a non-concentration assumption. Nets Katz later gave an alternative argument which appeared in[WIS05]that requires the condition given in Definition1.10. It is trivial to check that both of these conditions hold for the standard Kakeya problem. However, we will see that for generalϕ\phi-Kakeya problems, whether Definitions1.10and1.11hold starts to be a very interesting problem, and we will see that Definition1.10always implies Definition1.11, but the reverse direction is not always true (see Theorem1.33below).


Let us remark on the formulation of Definition1.10. The way in which this definition is formulated is not “local” and may not be easy to check. This will be addressed as part of our main theorem (Theorem1.33below), where we will give several equivalent characterizations for Katz-Wolff conditions. In particular, we will give a characterization similar to how Bourgain’s condition is formulated in Definition1.6.

## 1.4General curved Kakeya problems and their Bourgain’s conditions

In Subsection1.2, we statedϕ\phi-Kakeya sets andϕ\phi-Kakeya maximal estimates. To introduce these objects, one always needs to start with a phase functionϕ\phi. In the current paper, we will introduce a more general and natural way of stating curved Kakeya problems.


Letn≥2n\geq 2and letΦ:ℝn−1×ℝ×ℝn−1→ℝn\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}(1.21)

be a smooth map. We use(ω,t;ξ)(\omega,t;\xi)to denote the variables ofΦ\Phi. We always write vectors in column forms, unless otherwise specified.

## Definition 1.12(Φ\Phi-Kakeya sets andΦ\Phi-Kakeya maximal function).

We say that a Borel setE⊂ℝnE\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}withℒn​(E)=0\mathcal{L}^{n}(E)=0is aΦ\Phi-Kakeya set if there existsεΦ>0\varepsilon_{\Phi}>0such that for everyξ∈𝔹εΦn−1\xi\in\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{\Phi}}^{n-1}, there existsω∈𝔹εΦn−1\omega\in\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{\Phi}}^{n-1}satisfyingΦ​(ω,t;ξ)∈E​for every​|t|≤εΦ.\Phi(\omega,t;\xi)\in E\ \text{for every}\ |t|\leq\varepsilon_{\Phi}.(1.22)

We defineΦ\Phi-Kakeya maximal functions similarly toϕ\phi-Kakeya maximal functions.

If we takeΦ​(ω,t;ξ)=(ω+t​ξ,t),\Phi(\omega,t;\xi)=(\omega+t\xi,t),(1.23)

then we see the standard Kakeya problem.


Similarly to Hörmander’s condition in Definition1.5for Hörmander-type oscillatory integrals, we also introduce Hörmander’s condition forΦ\Phi-Kakeya sets.

## Definition 1.13(Hörmander’s condition forΦ\Phi-Kakeya sets).

LetΩ⊂ℝn−1×ℝ×ℝn−1\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}be an open set.
LetΦ:Ω→ℝn\Phi:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n}be a smooth map. We say thatΦ\Phisatisfies Hörmander’s condition at(ω,t;ξ)∈Ω(\omega,t;\xi)\in\Omegaif both of the following two conditions are satisfied:
- (1)

the followingn×nn\times nmatrix∇(ω,t)Φ​(ω,t;ξ)\nabla_{(\omega,t)}\Phi(\omega,t;\xi)(1.24)

is non-degenerate;
- (2)

the following2​n×2​n2n\times 2nmatrix[0∇(ω,t)Φ∇ξΦ∂tΦ∇(ω,t)​∂tΦ∇ξ​∂tΦ]\begin{bmatrix}0&\nabla_{(\omega,t)}\Phi&\nabla_{\xi}\Phi\\
\partial_{t}\Phi&\nabla_{(\omega,t)}\partial_{t}\Phi&\nabla_{\xi}\partial_{t}\Phi\end{bmatrix}(1.25)

is non-degenerate at(ω,t;ξ)(\omega,t;\xi).

We say thatΦ\Phisatisfies Hörmander’s condition onΩ\Omegaif it satisfies Hörmander’s condition at every point inΩ\Omega.

It is elementary to check thatΦ​(ω,t;ξ)=(ω+t​ξ,t)\Phi(\omega,t;\xi)=(\omega+t\xi,t)satisfies this Hörmander’s condition.


LetΦ\Phisatisfy Hörmander’s condition in Definition1.13. Since∇(ω,t)Φ​(ω,t;ξ)\nabla_{(\omega,t)}\Phi(\omega,t;\xi)is non-degenerate, it is elementary to see that we can writeΦ​(ω,t;ξ)=(X​(ω,t;ξ),t)\Phi(\omega,t;\xi)=\left(X(\omega,t;\xi),t\right)(1.26)

after a change of coordinates, whereX:ℝn−1×ℝ×ℝn−1→ℝn−1X:\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n-1}(1.27)

is a smooth map. Then Hörmander’s condition in Definition1.13is equivalent to the following condition.222The equivalence is elementary to see, and we leave out the details.

## Definition 1.14(Hörmander’s condition forXX).

LetΦ​(ω,t;ξ)=(X​(ω,t;ξ),t)\Phi(\omega,t;\xi)=\left(X(\omega,t;\xi),t\right). We sayXXsatisfies Hörmander’s condition at(ω,t;ξ)(\omega,t;\xi)if both of the following two conditions are satisfied:
- (1)

the following(n−1)×(n−1)(n-1)\times(n-1)matrix∇ωX​(ω,t;ξ)\nabla_{\omega}X(\omega,t;\xi)(1.28)

is non-degenerate;
- (2)

the following2​(n−1)×2​(n−1)2(n-1)\times 2(n-1)matrix[∇ωX∇ξX∇ω​∂tX∇ξ​∂tX]\begin{bmatrix}\nabla_{\omega}X&\nabla_{\xi}X\\
\nabla_{\omega}\partial_{t}X&\nabla_{\xi}\partial_{t}X\end{bmatrix}(1.29)

is non-degenerate at(ω,t;ξ)(\omega,t;\xi).

We now show that if a phase functionϕ\phisatisfies Hörmander’s condition in Definition1.5, then itsϕ\phi-Kakeya (maximal) problem can be written as aΦ\Phi-Kakeya (maximal) problem for some mapΦ\Phisatisfying Hörmander’s condition in Definition1.13. We do this exercise here to help us develop some intuition for Definition1.13and Definition1.14.

Letϕ​(x,t;ξ)\phi(x,t;\xi)be a phase function satisfying Hörmander’s condition in Definition1.5. Its characteristic curves are given byΦ​(w,t;ξ)=(X​(w,t;ξ),t)\Phi(w,t;\xi)=(X(w,t;\xi),t)(1.30)

whereX​(w,t;ξ)X(w,t;\xi)satisfies∇ξϕ​(X​(w,t;ξ),t;ξ)=w.\nabla_{\xi}\phi(X(w,t;\xi),t;\xi)=w.(1.31)

We show that the mapXXsatisfies Definition1.14.

To see (1.28), we differentiate both sides of (1.31) inwwand obtain∇x∇ξ⁡ϕ​(X​(w,t;ξ),t;ξ)​∇wX​(w,t;ξ)=In−1.\nabla_{x}\nabla_{\xi}\phi(X(w,t;\xi),t;\xi)\nabla_{w}X(w,t;\xi)=I_{n-1}.(1.32)

That∇wX\nabla_{w}Xis non-degenerate follows from the condition (H1) in Definition1.5. That (1.29) holds can be proven similarly and we leave out the details.

Moreover, it is very easy to find
mapsΦ\Phisuch thatΦ\Phi-Kakeya sets cannot be realized asϕ\phi-Kakeya sets for any phase functionϕ\phi, for instance

## Example 1.15.

LetΦ​(ω,t;ξ)=(X​(ω,t;ξ),t)\Phi(\omega,t;\xi)=(X(\omega,t;\xi),t)withX​(ω,t;ξ)=(ω1−t​ξ1−t2​ξ1​ξ2−t3​ξ2,ω2−t​ξ2−t2​ξ1​ξ2−t3​ξ1).X(\omega,t;\xi)=(\omega_{1}-t\xi_{1}-t^{2}\xi_{1}\xi_{2}-t^{3}\xi_{2},\omega_{2}-t\xi_{2}-t^{2}\xi_{1}\xi_{2}-t^{3}\xi_{1}).(1.33)

The key missing symmetry here is that∇ξ2ϕ\nabla^{2}_{\xi}\phiis a symmetric matrix, but no analogous conditions are needed forΦ\Phi-Kakeya problems. Indeed, curved Kakeya problems that do not come from phase functions are “generic” among all curved Kakeya problems.


For everyXXsatisfying the Hörmander’s condition in Definition1.14, we can transform it into a normal form (see Theorem1.18below). Throughout the paper, we do not distinguish the normal form ofΦ\Phifrom the normal form ofXX. In other words, whenever we say the normal form ofΦ\Phi, we always mean that we first findXXas above and then reduceXXto its normal form.

## Definition 1.16(Normal forms forΦ\Phi-Kakeya problems).

LetΦ​(ω,t;ξ)=(X​(ω,t;ξ),t)\Phi(\omega,t;\xi)=\left(X(\omega,t;\xi),t\right)withXXsatisfying the Hörmander’s condition in Definition1.14. We sayΦ\Phiis of a normal form, orXXis of a normal form, ifX​(ω,t;ξ)=ω+t​(ξ+O​(|ω|​|ξ|))+t2​O​(|ξ|)+t3​O​(|ξ|)+⋯,X(\omega,t;\xi)=\omega+t(\xi+O(|\omega||\xi|))+t^{2}O(|\xi|)+t^{3}O(|\xi|)+\cdots,(1.34)

whereO​(|ξ|)O(|\xi|)andO​(|ω|​|ξ|)O(|\omega||\xi|)are functions ofω,ξ\omega,\xi.

Analogously to the study of Hörmander-type oscillatory integrals and related curved Kakeya problems, Hörmander’s condition in Definition1.13does not guarantee thatΦ\Phi-Kakeya sets have full Hausdorff dimensions. For this purpose, we introduce Bourgain’s condition forΦ\Phi-Kakeya sets.

## Definition 1.17(Bourgain’s condition for normal forms ofΦ\Phi-Kakeya problems).

Consider a normal formΦ​(ω,t;ξ)=(X​(ω,t;ξ),t)\Phi(\omega,t;\xi)=\left(X(\omega,t;\xi),t\right). We sayΦ\Phi(orXX) satisfies Bourgain’s condition at the origin, if∇ξ​∂t2X​(ω,t;ξ)​is a multiple of​∇ξ​∂tX​(ω,t;ξ),\nabla_{\xi}\partial^{2}_{t}X(\omega,t;\xi)\text{ is a multiple of }\nabla_{\xi}\partial_{t}X(\omega,t;\xi),(1.35)

at(ω,t;ξ)=(0,0;0)(\omega,t;\xi)=(0,0;0). Similarly, we define Bourgain’s condition at a general point(ω0,t0;ξ0)(\omega_{0},t_{0};\xi_{0}).

We conclude this subsection by stating a few results that are completely parallel to those forϕ\phi-Kakeya (maximal) problems.

## Theorem 1.18.

LetΦ\Phibe a map satisfying Hörmander’s condition in Definition1.13. Let the mapXXbe given by (1.26).
- 1.

IfΦ\Phifails Bourgain’s condition in Definition1.17at some point, then there existspΦ<np_{\Phi}<nsuch that the followingΦ\Phi-Kakeya maximal estimate fails for everypΦ<p≤np_{\Phi}<p\leq n:‖𝒦δ(Φ)​f‖Lp​(𝔹ϵΦn−1)≲ϵ,Φ,pδ1−np−ϵ​‖f‖Lp​(ℝn),\big\|\mathcal{K}^{(\Phi)}_{\delta}f\big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}^{n-1}_{\epsilon_{\Phi}})}\lesssim_{\epsilon,\Phi,p}\delta^{1-\frac{n}{p}-\epsilon}\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},(1.36)

for all0<δ<ϵΦ0<\delta<\epsilon_{\Phi}and allϵ>0\epsilon>0.
- 2.

To proveLpL^{p}bounds for theΦ\Phi-Kakeya maximal function, it is equivalent to prove the same bounds forΦ\Phiof a normal form at the origin. In other words, one can apply changes of coordinates to turnΦ\Phiinto a normal form at the origin.
- 3.

The mapXXsatisfies Bourgain’s condition in Definition1.17at a point(w,t;ξ)(w,t;\xi)if and only if there existsc=c​(w,t;ξ)c=c(w,t;\xi)such thatrank​[∇ωX∇ξX0∂t∇ωX∂t∇ξX∇ωX∂t2∇ωX∂t2∇ξXc​∇ωX+2​∂t∇ωX]=2​(n−1),\displaystyle{\rm rank}\begin{bmatrix}\nabla_{\omega}X&\nabla_{\xi}X&0\\
\partial_{t}\nabla_{\omega}X&\partial_{t}\nabla_{\xi}X&\nabla_{\omega}X\\
\partial_{t}^{2}\nabla_{\omega}X&\partial_{t}^{2}\nabla_{\xi}X&c\nabla_{\omega}X+2\ \partial_{t}\nabla_{\omega}X\end{bmatrix}=2(n-1),(1.37)

if and only if there existsc=c​(w,t;ξ)c=c(w,t;\xi)such that∂t2((∇ωX)−1​∇ξX)​(w,t;ξ)=c​∂t((∇ωX)−1​∇ξX)​(w,t;ξ).\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}((\nabla_{\omega}X)^{-1}\nabla_{\xi}X)(w,t;\xi)=c\ \partial_{t}((\nabla_{\omega}X)^{-1}\nabla_{\xi}X)(w,t;\xi).(1.38)
- 4.

Letϕ\phibe a phase function satisfying Hörmander’s condition in Definition1.5. DefineXXvia (1.31).
Ifϕ\phisatisfies Bourgain’s condition, thenXXalso satisfies Bourgain’s condition.
- 5.

IfΦ\Phisatisfies Bourgain’s condition in Definition1.17everywhere, then theΦ\Phi-Kakeya problem satisfies polynomial Wolff axioms333It is not our main focus to discuss polynomial Wolff axioms here, and we refer the definition to Theorem 1.2 in[GWZ24].

The proof for Theorem1.18is routine and tedious. As the paper is already long, we decide to leave out the proofs.

Readers familiar with the formulation of Bourgain’s condition for phase function in[GWZ24]will find the form (1.38) very familiar as well, and very easy to accept. The reason that we provide another equivalent formulation (1.37) is that it is extremely convenient when applied to the study of Schrödinger equations with potentials, see AppendixB.

Below let us try to make the second item in Theorem1.18more precise. We make explicit the transformations we may apply to a family of curves to get the same Kakeya problem. Write𝒞\mathcal{C}for a2​(n−1)2(n-1)-parameter family of curves, andℓξ,w\ell_{\xi,w}for a curve in the family. Write the parameter space asΣ×W\Sigma\times W, and the space in which the curves live asMM. Consider a mapF=(FΣ×W,FM),F=(F_{\Sigma\times W},F_{M}),(1.39)

factoring into local diffeomorphism onΣ×W\Sigma\times WandMMrespectively. The mapFFinduces a new family of curves𝒞′=FM​(𝒞)\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=F_{M}(\mathcal{C})pointwise:
A curveℓξ,w∈𝒞\ell_{\xi,w}\in\mathcal{C}is sent toℓξ′,w′′=FM​(ℓFΣ×W−1​(ξ′,w′))∈FM​(𝒞).\ell^{\prime}_{\xi^{\prime},w^{\prime}}=F_{M}(\ell_{F^{-1}_{\Sigma\times W}(\xi^{\prime},w^{\prime})})\in F_{M}(\mathcal{C}).(1.40)

## Definition 1.19(Direction-equivalence).

A direction-preserving transformation is a transformationF​(ξ,w,𝐱)=(FΣ×W​(ξ,w),FM​(𝐱))F(\xi,w,{\bf x})=(F_{\Sigma\times W}(\xi,w),F_{M}({\bf x}))(1.41)

which in addition satisfiesFΣ×W​(ξ,w)=(FΣ​(ξ),FW​(ξ,w)).\displaystyle F_{\Sigma\times W}(\xi,w)=(F_{\Sigma}(\xi),F_{W}(\xi,w)).(1.42)

We say that𝒞\mathcal{C}is direction-equivalent to𝒞′\mathcal{C}^{\prime}if there exists a direction-preserving transformation.

The key Kakeya-hypothesis preserved under direction-preserving transformations is, of course, direction separatedness. The Katz-Wolff condition is preserved under direction-equivalence.


We should remark on another more restrictive notion of equivalence at the level of phase functions.
Writeℓξ,w\ell_{\xi,w}for the characteristic curve{𝐱:∇ξϕ​(𝐱,ξ)=w}.\{{\bf x}:\nabla_{\xi}\phi({\bf x},\xi)=w\}.(1.43)

We may apply diffeomorphisms𝐱′=G1​(𝐱){\bf x}^{\prime}=G_{1}({\bf x})andξ′=G2​(ξ)\xi^{\prime}=G_{2}(\xi)separately to a phase functionϕ\phiand obtainϕ′​(𝐱′,ξ′)=ϕ​(G1−1​(𝐱′),G2−1​(ξ′)).\phi^{\prime}({\bf x}^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})=\phi(G_{1}^{-1}({\bf x}^{\prime}),G_{2}^{-1}(\xi^{\prime})).(1.44)

A quick calculation relates the characteristic curvesℓξ′,w′′\ell^{\prime}_{\xi^{\prime},w^{\prime}}ofϕ′\phi^{\prime}to those ofϕ\phi:ℓξ′,w′′=G1​(ℓξ,∇G2​(ξ)​w′).\displaystyle\ell^{\prime}_{\xi^{\prime},w^{\prime}}=G_{1}(\ell_{\xi,\nabla G_{2}(\xi)w^{\prime}}).(1.45)

We see that at the level of curves, the corresponding mapFFis given by:F​(ξ,w,𝐱)\displaystyle F(\xi,w,{\bf x})=(FΣ×W​(ξ,w),FM​(𝐱))\displaystyle=(F_{\Sigma\times W}(\xi,w),F_{M}({\bf x}))(1.46)=((G2​(ξ),∇G2​(ξ)−1​w),G1​(𝐱)).\displaystyle=((G_{2}(\xi),\nabla G_{2}(\xi)^{-1}w),G_{1}({\bf x})).(1.47)

This is a direction-preserving transformation with extra restrictions.

## 1.5Finer characterizations forΦ\Phi-Kakeya sets

In Subsection1.3, we introduced finer characterizations for Hörmander-type phases. In the current subsection, we introduce parallel finer characterizations for the general curved Kakeya problems (Φ\Phi-Kakeya problems) in Subsection1.4, see Definition1.20and Definition1.21below. Moreover, we will also introduce a few new notions, motivated by various examples, and we will see eventually how all these notions fit together in our main theorem (see Theorem1.33).

## Definition 1.20(Katz-Wolff condition for curved Kakeya problems).

Consider theΦ\Phi-Kakeya problem whereΦ​(w,t;ξ)=(X​(w,t;ξ),t),\Phi(w,t;\xi)=(X(w,t;\xi),t),(1.48)

and without loss of generality we assume thatXXis in a normal form.
Fixw0w_{0}andξ′≠ξ′′\xi^{\prime}\neq\xi^{\prime\prime}, and consider two characteristic curves{(X​(w0,t;ξ′),t):|t|≤ϵΦ},{(X​(w0,t;ξ′′),t):|t|≤ϵΦ}.\{(X(w_{0},t;\xi^{\prime}),t):|t|\leq\epsilon_{\Phi}\},\ \ \{(X(w_{0},t;\xi^{\prime\prime}),t):|t|\leq\epsilon_{\Phi}\}.(1.49)

For givent1,t2t_{1},t_{2}, letw​(t1,t2)w(t_{1},t_{2})andξ​(t1,t2)\xi(t_{1},t_{2})be the unique (if exist) solutions satisfying that the characteristic curve{(X​(w​(t1,t2),t;ξ​(t1,t2)),t):|t|≤ϵΦ}\{(X(w(t_{1},t_{2}),t;\xi(t_{1},t_{2})),t):|t|\leq\epsilon_{\Phi}\}(1.50)

passes through the two points(X​(w0,t1;ξ′),t1),(X​(w0,t2;ξ′′),t2).(X(w_{0},t_{1};\xi^{\prime}),t_{1}),\ \ (X(w_{0},t_{2};\xi^{\prime\prime}),t_{2}).(1.51)

We say thatΦ\Phisatisfies the Katz-Wolff condition if for all choices ofw0,ξ′≠ξ′′w_{0},\xi^{\prime}\neq\xi^{\prime\prime}, the image ofξ​(t1,t2)\xi(t_{1},t_{2})is a curve (one-dimensional instead of two-dimensional). Equivalently,rank​[∂t1ξ​(t1,t2),∂t2ξ​(t1,t2)]<2,∀t1,t2.\mathrm{rank}[\partial_{t_{1}}\xi(t_{1},t_{2}),\partial_{t_{2}}\xi(t_{1},t_{2})]<2,\ \ \forall t_{1},t_{2}.(1.52)

## Definition 1.21(Totally geodesic condition for curved Kakeya problems).

Under the same notation as in Definition1.20,
we say thatΦ\Phisatisfies
the totally geodesic condition if for all choices ofw0w_{0}andξ′≠ξ′′\xi^{\prime}\neq\xi^{\prime\prime}, the set⋃t1,t2{(X​(w​(t1,t2),t;ξ​(t1,t2)),t):|t|≤ϵΦ}\bigcup_{t_{1},t_{2}}\{(X(w(t_{1},t_{2}),t;\xi(t_{1},t_{2})),t):|t|\leq\epsilon_{\Phi}\}(1.53)

forms a two-dimensional surface (instead of a 3-dimensional region). Moreover, for a phase functionϕ\phisatisfying Hörmander’s condition in Definition1.5, we say that it satisfies the totally geodesic condition if its inducedΦ\Phi(given by (1.31)) satisfies the totally geodesic condition.

Recall Bourgain’s example (1.9) in[BOU91]. We write it as aΦ\Phi-Kakeya problem, withΦ​(w,t;ξ)=(w1+t​ξ2+t2​ξ1,w2+t​ξ1,t),\Phi(w,t;\xi)=(w_{1}+t\xi_{2}+t^{2}\xi_{1},w_{2}+t\xi_{1},t),(1.54)

wherew=(w1,w2)w=(w_{1},w_{2})andξ=(ξ1,ξ2)\xi=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}).
We apply a change of coordinate and see thatΦ\Phiis direction-equivalent toΦ~​(w,t;ξ)=(w1−t​w2+t​ξ2,w2+t​ξ1,t).\widetilde{\Phi}(w,t;\xi)=(w_{1}-tw_{2}+t\xi_{2},w_{2}+t\xi_{1},t).(1.55)

From this, it is elementary to see that bothΦ\PhiandΦ~\widetilde{\Phi}satisfy the totally geodesic condition in Definition1.21. Moreover, every totally geodesic surface gives rise to a Kakeya set (see Subsection2.3below).

## Definition 1.22(Extra-worst compression condition).

Taken=3n=3.
We say thatΦ\Phi(or a phase functionϕ\phi) satisfies the extra-worst compression condition ifΦ\Phi(or the induced mapΦ\Phigiven by (1.31)) satisfies the totally geodesic condition in Definition1.21, and every totally geodesic surface gives rise to aΦ\Phi-Kakeya set.

The mapsΦ\Phisatisfying Definition1.22are in some sense “extra-worst possible” maps, and there are plenty of low dimensional Kakeya sets associated to theseΦ\Phi. In comparison, in the following definition, we introduce “worst possible” maps, meaning that we can find at least one low dimensional Kakeya set.

## Definition 1.23(Worst compression condition).

Taken=3n=3and we follow the notation in Definition1.20and Definition1.21. We say thatΦ\Phi(or a phase functionϕ\phi) satisfies the worst compression condition if
we can findw0w_{0}andξ′≠ξ′′\xi^{\prime}\neq\xi^{\prime\prime}satisfying that
(1.53) forms a two-dimensional surface andrank​[∂t1ξ​(t1,t2),∂t2ξ​(t1,t2)]=2,\mathrm{rank}[\partial_{t_{1}}\xi(t_{1},t_{2}),\partial_{t_{2}}\xi(t_{1},t_{2})]=2,(1.56)

for somet1≠t2t_{1}\neq t_{2}.

If a mapΦ\Phisatisfies the worst compression condition in Definition1.23, it is immediate to see that one can find aΦ\Phi-Kakeya set that is two dimensional. Moreover, we will show below in one of our main theorems (see Theorem1.34) that
this is the only way for a two-dimensionalΦ\Phi-Kakeya set to occur, under the extra assumption thatΦ\Phiis semi-algebraic; we will also prove the above result more quantitatively, meaning that ifΦ\Phifails the worst compression condition, then we can findεΦ>0\varepsilon_{\Phi}>0such that everyΦ\Phi-Kakeya set has dimension≥2+εΦ\geq 2+\varepsilon_{\Phi}.


MapsΦ\Phisatisfying the extra-worst compression condition are extremely rigid; for instance, we will show in our main theorem (see Theorem1.33below) thatΦ\Phisatisfies the extra-worst condition if and only if it satisfies the totally geodesic condition in Definition1.21and fails Bourgain’s condition. However, maps satisfying the worst compression condition are less rare, and it is very easy to find maps satisfying the worst compression condition but not the extra-worst compression condition.


In contrast to the above two definitions, the mapsΦ\Phisatisfying the following definition are the “best possible”, in the sense that their associated Kakeya sets are expected to have full dimensions.

## Definition 1.24(Bochner-Riesz-type).

We say that the mapΦ\Phi(or a phase functionϕ\phi) is of Bochner-Riesz-type if it (or the induced mapΦ\Phigiven by (1.31)) is direction-equivalent to the characteristic curves of the phase functionϕ​(x,t;ξ)=|(x,t)−(ξ,φ​(ξ))|,\phi(x,t;\xi)=|(x,t)-(\xi,\varphi(\xi))|,(1.57)

whereφ:ℝn−1→ℝ\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\to\mathbb{R}is a smooth function. The curves are given byℓξ,w​(t)=(ξ,φ​(ξ))−t​(w,1).\ell_{\xi,w}(t)=(\xi,\varphi(\xi))-t(w,1).(1.58)

The connections among the newly introduced definitions in this subsection will become clear once we state our main theorem (see Theorem1.33below).

## 1.6The geometry of paths and other geometry background

We are interested in putting a geometric structure onM=ℝnM=\mathbb{R}^{n}whose geodesics are exactly the curves induced byΦ\Phi(which we can callΦ\Phi-curves)–this will give us access to geometric invariants that we can use to study our Kakeya set.
Riemannian geometry is unfortunately too restrictive (see[DGG+24]) to encode the family ofΦ\Phi-curves for mostΦ\Phi, so we need to work instead with a more general structure. The structure we use is aspray space. In this section we include the minimal definitions needed to state and motivate our results. See AppendixAfor further details. Most of the materials in this subsection are standard, and can found for instance in the book[SHE01].


First we writeMMfor a manifold andT​MTMfor its tangent bundle, with projectionπT​M:T​M→M.\pi_{TM}:TM\to M.(1.59)

IfVVis a vector space, we sayC⊂V∖0C\subset V\setminus 0isconicif for allλ>0\lambda>0,λ​C=C\lambda C=C(these can be identified with subsets of(V∖0)/ℝ>0(V\setminus 0)/\mathbb{R}_{>0}, or with an inner product chosen, subsets ofSn−1S^{n-1}). A set𝒰⊂T​M∖0\mathcal{U}\subset TM\setminus 0(1.60)

is a fiberwise conic set if for eachx∈Mx\in M,𝒰x:=𝒰∩Tx​M⊂Tx​M∖0\mathcal{U}_{x}:=\mathcal{U}\cap T_{x}M\subset T_{x}M\setminus 0(1.61)

is conic.

## Definition 1.25(Spray space).

A spray space is a triple(M,𝒰,G)(M,\mathcal{U},G)of a manifoldMM, an open fiberwise conic set𝒰⊂T​M∖0\mathcal{U}\subset TM\setminus 0, and𝐆∈Γ​(T​𝒰)\mathbf{G}\in\Gamma(T\mathcal{U})expressed in the local coordinates(xi,yi)(x^{i},y^{i})in𝒰\mathcal{U}as𝐆=yi​∂∂xi−2​Gi​(x,y)​∂∂yi,\displaystyle\mathbf{G}=y^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}-2G^{i}(x,y)\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}},(1.62)

whereGi​(x,y)G^{i}(x,y)are local functions on𝒰\mathcal{U}satisfyingGi​(x,λ​y)=λ2​Gi​(x,y)G^{i}(x,\lambda y)=\lambda^{2}G^{i}(x,y)(1.63)

for everyλ>0\lambda>0.444This is a modification of the definition of a spray space from[SHE01]. All the definitions and theorems about spray spaces that we use are applied locally, so there is no issue in working on a fiberwise conic section instead of the entire slit tangent bundleT​M∖0TM\setminus 0.

As the introduction is already long, we will postpone the discussion of a geometric interpretation of spray spaces to Section3. For readers not familiar with spray spaces, it may be a good idea to take a look at the first half of Section3first, and this may help understand the contents of the forthcoming materials, say Proposition1.26.


In what follows, the objects in bold are coordinate invariant.
Thegeodesicsof𝐆\bf Gare the projections toMMof the integral curves of𝐆\bf G. That is, the geodesicsγ\gammasatisfy the equationc¨i+2​Gi​(c,c˙)=0.\ddot{c}^{i}+2G^{i}(c,\dot{c})=0.(1.64)

We refer to Section3(in particular, equation (3.9)) for
how to derive the equation (1.64) and for its geometric interpretation in local coordinates. It is natural to work with coordinate invariant objects, since Kakeya estimates are essentially invariant with respect to diffeomorphism (of course not depending on scale parameters likeδ\delta).
The proposition below says that families ofΦ\Phi-curves and the geodesics of a spray space are essentially equivalent (after remembering the identifications of parameters).

## Proposition 1.26.

Letn≥2n\geq 2and letΦ\Phibe a smooth map satisfying Hörmander’s condition as in Definition1.13. LetεΦ>0\varepsilon_{\Phi}>0be a sufficiently small constant depending onΦ\Phi. Then there is a spray space(M,𝒰,𝐆)(M,\mathcal{U},\mathbf{G})and a map(W,Ξ):𝒰→𝔹εΦn−1×𝔹εΦn−1,(W,\Xi):\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{\mathbb{B}}^{n-1}_{\varepsilon_{\Phi}}\times\mathbb{B}^{n-1}_{\varepsilon_{\Phi}},(1.65)

where we write(Wx,Ξx):𝒰x→𝔹εΦn−1×𝔹εΦn−1(W_{x},\Xi_{x}):\mathcal{U}_{x}\to\mathbb{B}^{n-1}_{\varepsilon_{\Phi}}\times\mathbb{B}^{n-1}_{\varepsilon_{\Phi}}(1.66)

with the following properties:
The geodesic with tangentvx∈𝒰xv_{x}\in\mathcal{U}_{x}is the curve with parameters(Wx​(vx),Ξx​(vx)).(W_{x}(v_{x}),\Xi_{x}(v_{x})).(1.67)

Conversely, every curve with parameters in𝔹εΦn−1×𝔹εΦn−1\mathbb{B}^{n-1}_{\varepsilon_{\Phi}}\times\mathbb{B}^{n-1}_{\varepsilon_{\Phi}}is a geodesic of the spray space.

Proposition1.26is stated in a coordinate-free form. When we prove this proposition in Section3, we will first write it in local coordinates, and readers who are not used to the language of spray spaces can read the formulation there directly.

## Example 1.27(Flat spray).

A spray(M,𝒰,𝐆)(M,\mathcal{U},\mathbf{G})is flat if there are local coordinates onMMwhere𝐆=yi​∂∂xi\mathbf{G}=y^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}. The geodesics are the family of lines.

When studyingΦ\Phi-Kakeya sets, we may always localize to a positive fraction of the curves and a smaller fraction of space (of course not depending on a scale parameter likeδ\delta). In the language of spray spaces, this gives us the freedom to shrinkMMand𝒰\mathcal{U}to smaller open sets around our point of interest. For example, after fixing(x0,y0)∈𝒰(x_{0},y_{0})\in\mathcal{U}and a local trivialization ofT​MTM, we can findU⊂MU\subset Mcontainingx0x_{0}and an open coneC⊂ℝnC\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}containingy0y_{0}such thatU×C⊂𝒰U\times C\subset\mathcal{U}. It will sometimes be useful to restrict to this product set.

We of course are only interested in studyingΦ\Phi-Kakeya sets as point sets (we do not care how the curves are parameterized). This is captured by the following definition.

## Definition 1.28(Pointwise projectively related).

Two sprays𝐆\mathbf{G}and𝐆~\tilde{\mathbf{G}}defined on(M,𝒰)(M,\mathcal{U})are pointwise projectively related if for any geodesicc​(t)c(t)of𝐆\mathbf{G}, there is an orientation-preserving reparameterizationt=t​(s)t=t(s)such thatc​(s):=c​(t​(s))c(s):=c(t(s))is a geodesic of𝐆~\tilde{\mathbf{G}}, and vice versa. Equivalently, there is a scalar functionPPon𝒰\mathcal{U}which is positively11-homogeneous inyysuch that𝐆~=𝐆−2​P​𝐘,\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{G}}=\mathbf{G}-2P\mathbf{Y},(1.68)

where in coordinates𝐘=yi​∂∂yi\mathbf{Y}=y^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}.

There are two natural projective invariants for𝐆\mathbf{G}. The first is theDouglas tensor𝐃∈Γ​(𝒰,hom⁡((π∗​T​M)⊗3,π∗​T​M)),\mathbf{D}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{U},\hom((\pi^{*}TM)^{\otimes 3},\pi^{*}TM)),(1.69)

which detects if𝐆\mathbf{G}isaffineup to projective transformation (this is a non-Riemannian quantity). The other is theWeyl tensor𝐖∈Γ​(𝒰,End​(π∗​T​M)),\mathbf{W}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathrm{End}(\pi^{*}TM)),(1.70)

which detects curvature up to projective transformation. The tensor𝐖\mathbf{W}is the projective trace-free part of the Riemann tensor𝐑∈Γ​(𝒰,End​(π∗​T​M)).\mathbf{R}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathrm{End}(\pi^{*}TM)).(1.71)

We will give formulas for all these objects in AppendixAand their properties. These invariants let us detect whether a spray𝐆\mathbf{G}is (locally) projectively flat fordim​M≥3\text{dim}M\geq 3(that is, there are coordinates onMMwhere𝐆\mathbf{G}is projectively related to the flat sprayyi​∂∂xiy^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}).

## Definition 1.29(Projectively flat).

A spray space(M,𝒰,𝐆)(M,\mathcal{U},\mathbf{G})is (locally) projectively flat if
any(x0,y0)∈𝒰(x_{0},y_{0})\in\mathcal{U}has a neighborhood𝒰′⊂𝒰\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{U}on which𝐆\mathbf{G}is projectively related to a flat spray.

Let us formulate Definition1.29in the setting ofΦ\Phi-Kakeya sets. AΦ\Phi-Kakeya problem is said to beprojectively flatif
one can find a local diffeomorphismFFacting onℝn\mathbb{R}^{n}such that
the image of{Φ​(w,t;ξ):|t|≤εΦ}\{\Phi(w,t;\xi):|t|\leq\varepsilon_{\Phi}\}(1.72)

under the mapFFis a line segment, for everyw,ξw,\xi.

## Theorem 1.30(Minor modification of[SHE01, Theorem 13.5.1]).

Let(M,𝒰,𝐆)(M,\mathcal{U},\mathbf{G})be a spray space withdim​M≥3\text{dim}M\geq 3. Suppose that𝐃≡0,𝐖≡0.\mathbf{D}\equiv 0,\ \ \mathbf{W}\equiv 0.(1.73)

Then𝐆\mathbf{G}is locally projectively flat.

To see that this holds, one uses𝐃=0\mathbf{D}=0to pass to an affine spray defined globally, which agrees with𝐆\mathbf{G}on an open𝒰′⊂𝒰\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{U}. Then apply[SHE01, Theorem 13.5.1].


It was pointed out in[GLX25]that
the Kakeya problem on Riemannian manifolds of constant sectional curvature can be turned into the Kakeya problem on the Euclidean space. If we use the language of spray spaces, then the above result in[GLX25]can be summarized as: The Kakeya problem on Riemannian manifolds of constant sectional curvature is projectively flat. This is a special case of Theorem1.30, as Douglas curvature already vanishes on a Riemannian manifold, and constant curvature is the same as vanishing Weyl curvature in the setting of Riemannian geometry.


Let us introduce the notion of a totally geodesic surface for a spray space.

## Definition 1.31.

Let(M,𝒰,𝐆)(M,\mathcal{U},\mathbf{G})be a spray space. We say that a smooth surfaceS⊂MS\subset Mis totally geodesic if for anyx∈Sx\in Sand geodesicγ\gammasatisfyingγ​(0)=x\gamma(0)=xandγ˙​(0)∈Tx​S∩𝒰x,\dot{\gamma}(0)\in T_{x}S\cap\mathcal{U}_{x},(1.74)

we haveγ​(t)∈S\gamma(t)\in Sforttin a neighborhood of0.

We may now introduce a version of Definition1.21adapted to spray spaces.

## Definition 1.32(Totally Geodesic condition: geometric version).

We say that(M,𝒰,𝐆)(M,\mathcal{U},\mathbf{G})has many totally geodesic surfaces if for eachx∈Mx\in Mand 2-planeΠ\Pithrough the origin withΠ∩𝒰x≠∅\Pi\cap\mathcal{U}_{x}\neq\emptyset, there is a totally geodesic surfaceSSsatisfyingTx​S=ΠT_{x}S=\Pi.

So far for a curved Kakeya problem, we have two totally geodesic conditions: Definition1.21and Definition1.32. Definition1.21is stated for curved Kakeya problems directly. For Definition1.32, we first apply Theorem1.26to our curved Kakeya problem to obtain a spray space; once we have a spray space, we can check Definition1.32.
Let us give a sketch that Definition1.21and Definition1.32are equivalent.

## Sketch of equivalence of Definition1.21and Definition1.32.

Suppose thatXXis as in Definition1.21. Consider the surfaceS​(y1,y2,𝐱0)S(y_{1},y_{2},\mathbf{x}_{0})formed by the two intersecting curvesΓy1​(𝐱0)\Gamma_{y_{1}}(\mathbf{x}_{0})andΓy2​(𝐱0)\Gamma_{y_{2}}(\mathbf{x}_{0}). Identify some interior pointxint​(y1,y2,𝐱0)x_{\mathrm{int}}(y_{1},y_{2},\mathbf{x}_{0})and compute the tangent planeP​(y1,y2,𝐱0)P(y_{1},y_{2},\mathbf{x}_{0})at that point. By the implicit function theorem, for anyxxin a sufficiently small neighborhood and tangent planePPintersecting a small enough open cone, there arey1,y2,𝐱0y_{1},y_{2},\mathbf{x}_{0}such thatx=xint​(y1,y2,𝐱0),P=P​(y1,y2,𝐱0).x=x_{\mathrm{int}}(y_{1},y_{2},\mathbf{x}_{0}),\ \ P=P(y_{1},y_{2},\mathbf{x}_{0}).(1.75)

ThenS=S​(y1,y2,𝐱0)S=S(y_{1},y_{2},\mathbf{x}_{0})is a totally geodesic surface nearxxin the sense of Definition (1.32).
Indeed, there is a 1-parameter family of curves inSSpassing through any given point. So for any direction (in a small enough open cone) tangent toSS, there is a curve inSSin that direction, and that curve is unique.

For the other direction, assume thatXXis an Definition1.32. Fix two intersecting curvesΓy1​(x0)\Gamma_{y_{1}}(x_{0})andΓy2​(x0)\Gamma_{y_{2}}(x_{0})(withy1y_{1}andy2y_{2}close enough), and consider the planeΠ\Piwhich contains their tangent vectors atx0x_{0}. By Definition1.32, there is a totally geodesic surfaceSSwithTx0​S=ΠT_{x_{0}}S=\Pi. These curves lie inSSlocally.
Now fix pointsx1∈Γy1​(x0)x_{1}\in\Gamma_{y_{1}}(x_{0})andx2∈Γy2​(x0)x_{2}\in\Gamma_{y_{2}}(x_{0}), both different fromx0x_{0}. Consider the 1-parameter family of (unit) tangent vectors inTx1​ST_{x_{1}}S. SinceSSis totally geodesic, these curves all lie inSS. By the implicit function theorem, one of these curves intersectsx2x_{2}(forx1,x2x_{1},x_{2}appropriately chosen in open subsets of the curvesΓy1​(x0)\Gamma_{y_{1}}(x_{0})andΓy2​(x0)\Gamma_{y_{2}}(x_{0})). Passing over allx1,x2x_{1},x_{2}in fixed open subsets of the curvesΓy1​(x0)\Gamma_{y_{1}}(x_{0})andΓy2​(x0)\Gamma_{y_{2}}(x_{0}), we get Definition1.21.

## 1.7Statement of main theorems

Before discussing the formal theorem statements in the next section, we give the picture at the level of curved Kakeya problems.
We abbreviate the definitions and make some small comments for the picture:
- •

K.W.: Katz-Wolff condition.
- •

T.G.: Totally geodesic condition.
- •

Flat: (Direction-equivalent to) projectively flat.
- •

B. Cond.: Bourgain condition.
- •

B.R.-type:Bochner-Riesz-type.
- •

extra-worst-comp.Extra-worst compression condition in Definition1.22.Figure 1:Main Venn Diagram of Conditions

Below we state the theorems that justify Figure1.

## Theorem 1.33(Main Venn-diagram Theorem).

LetΦ\Phibe a map satisfying Hörmander’s condition in Definition1.13and taken≥3n\geq 3.
- 1.

Taken=3n=3. ThatΦ\Phisatisfies the totally geodesic condition (either Definition1.32or Definition1.21) is equivalent to that the induced spray space (byΦ\Phias in Proposition1.26) is projectively flat.
- 2.

IfΦ\Phisatisfies the Katz-Wolff condition, then it also satisfies the totally geodesic condition.
- 3.

IfΦ\Phisatisfies the Katz-Wolff condition, then it also satisfies Bourgain’s condition.
- 4.

IfΦ\Phisatisfies both the totally geodesic condition and Bourgain’s condition, then it is of the Bochner-Riesz-type555We also refer to the beginning of Section6for a more quantitative version of this statement., which further implies that it satisfies the Katz-Wolff condition.
- 5.

Taken=3n=3. IfΦ\Phisatisfies the totally geodesic but fails Bourgain’s condition, then it satisfies the extra-worst compression condition.666For dimensionsn≥4n\geq 4, the worst compression will not just occur inside(n−1)(n-1)-dimensional sub-manifolds, but sub-manifolds of even smaller dimensions, see examples for instance in[GHI19]. Therefore whenn≥4n\geq 4, one will need new definitions that replace Definition1.22. We will leave this to future works.

Moreover, if we letϕ\phibe a phase function satisfying Hörmander’s condition in Definition1.5, then all the above statements hold true withϕ\phiin place ofΦ\Phi.

Examples in Subsection2.2show that the Katz-Wolff condition is strictly stronger than Bourgain’s condition. Moreover, recall the discussion around (1.54) and (1.55) that Bourgain’s example (1.9) satisfies the extra-worst compression condition.


As a consequence of
Theorem1.33, we immediately obtain the following several results.

The original definition for the totally geodesic condition in Definition1.21is not a local condition, and may not be easy to check. As a consequence of item 1 in Theorem1.33, we see that to check the totally geodesic condition, it is equivalent to write down the spray space (Proposition1.26), and then to check thatW=D=0W=D=0.

The Katz-Wolff condition in Definition1.20is not a local condition either. As a consequence of item 2 and item 3 in Theorem1.33, to check the Katz-Wolff condition, it is equivalent to check Bourgain’s condition, and then to check totally geodesic condition.


Theorem1.33discusses, in particular, a “best” possible behavior for curved Kakeya problems. These curved Kakeya problems satisfy the Katz-Wolff condition, which is equivalent to Bourgain’s condition together with the totally geodesic condition, and is further equivalent to the Bochner-Riesz type condition. One can apply the result of Wang and Zahl[WZ25]and obtain that forn=3n=3,Φ\Phi-Kakeya sets must have dimension 3 wheneverΦ\Phisatisfies any of the above conditions.


The next theorem characterizes allΦ\Phithat admit a two-dimensional Kakeya set, under the extra assumptions thatn=3n=3andΦ\Phiis semi-algebraic.

## Theorem 1.34.

Assumen=3n=3.
- 1.

Assume the mapΦ\Phiis semi-algebraic. ThenΦ\Phisatisfies the worst compression condition in Definition1.23if and only if
there exists aΦ\Phi-Kakeya set that is two dimensional. WheneverΦ\Phifails the worst compression condition, we can findκΦ>0\kappa_{\Phi}>0such that everyΦ\Phi-Kakeya set has dimension≥2+κΦ.\geq 2+\kappa_{\Phi}.
- 2.

Assume that the phase functionϕ\phiis semi-algebraic. Thenϕ\phifails the worst compression condition if and only if the following statement holds: For every2<p<∞2<p<\infty, there existsβϕ​(p)<1/p\beta_{\phi}(p)<1/psuch that‖𝒦δ(ϕ)​f‖Lp​(𝔹ϵϕn−1)≲ϕ,pδ−βϕ​(p)​‖f‖Lp​(ℝn),\big\|\mathcal{K}^{(\phi)}_{\delta}f\big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}^{n-1}_{\epsilon_{\phi}})}\lesssim_{\phi,p}\delta^{-\beta_{\phi}(p)}\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},(1.76)

for everyδ∈(0,ϵϕ)\delta\in(0,\epsilon_{\phi}). In particular, ifϕ\phifails the worst compression condition, then there existsκϕ>0\kappa_{\phi}>0such that everyϕ\phi-Kakeya set has dimension≥2+κϕ\geq 2+\kappa_{\phi}.

Note that the estimate (1.76) withp≥2p\geq 2andβϕ​(p)=1/p\beta_{\phi}(p)=1/pholds for every phase functionϕ\phi, see for instance equation (6.15) in[BOU91]and Proposition 1.4 in[CGG+25]. Moreover,[BOU91]and[CGG+25]gave several sufficient conditions for (1.76) to be true for everyp>2p>2and someβϕ​(p)<1/p\beta_{\phi}(p)<1/p; once such an estimate is proven (for somep>2p>2), a standard argument shows that everyϕ\phi-Kakeya set has dimension≥2+κϕ\geq 2+\kappa_{\phi}for someκϕ>0\kappa_{\phi}>0.

We use the exampleϕ​(x,t;y)=⟨x,y⟩+t2​(y12+y22)+t2​(y1​y2+16​y14)+t32​y12\phi(x,t;y)=\langle x,y\rangle+\frac{t}{2}\left(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}\right)+t^{2}\left(y_{1}y_{2}+\frac{1}{6}y_{1}^{4}\right)+\frac{t^{3}}{2}y_{1}^{2}(1.77)

to show that the conditions in[BOU91]and[CGG+25]are not necessary.
Let𝒵K​(ϕ)\mathcal{Z}_{K}(\phi)denote the set of ally∈𝔹2y\in\mathbb{B}^{2}for which there exist scalarsμi,j,1≤i,j≤2\mu_{i,j},1\leq i,j\leq 2such thatdet​∂y​y2ϕ​(0,t;y)=∑1≤i,j≤2μi,j​∂yi​yj2ϕ​(0,t;y)for all​t∈(−1,1).\operatorname{det}\partial_{yy}^{2}\phi(0,t;y)=\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq 2}\mu_{i,j}\partial_{y_{i}y_{j}}^{2}\phi(0,t;y)\quad\text{ for all }t\in(-1,1).(1.78)

Hypothesis I) in[CGG+25, Definition 2.2]requires thatℒ2​(𝒵K​(ϕ))=0.\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{K}(\phi))=0.(1.79)

Under this hypothesis,[CGG+25]proved that (1.76) holds for everyp>2p>2and someβϕ​(p)<1/p\beta_{\phi}(p)<1/p. However, it is elementary to check that the phase function (1.77) fails (1.79) and at the same time fails the worst compression condition as well, showing that (1.79) is not a necessary condition.


Once the necessary and sufficient condition is established in Theorem1.34, one can follow the standard argument in[BOU91, Section 5](see also[CGG+25, Proposition 2.10]) and immediately obtain the following corollary.

## Corollary 1.35.

Assume thatn=3n=3and the phase functionϕ\phiis elliptic and semi-algebraic. Thenϕ\phifails the worst compression condition if and only if there existsκϕ>0\kappa_{\phi}>0such that the estimate (1.3) holds, that is,‖TN(ϕ)​f‖Lp​(ℝn)≲ϕ,a,p,ϵN−np+ϵ​‖f‖Lp​(ℝn−1)\|T^{(\phi)}_{N}f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{\phi,a,p,\epsilon}N^{-\frac{n}{p}+\epsilon}\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}(1.80)

holds,
for allp≥10/3−κϕp\geq 10/3-\kappa_{\phi}.

## 1.8A coordinate invariant formulation of Bourgain’s condition

LetM⊂ℝnM\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}andΣ⊂ℝn−1\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-1}be open sets, andϕ:M×Σ→ℝ\phi:M\times\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}a phase function satisfying Bourgain’s condition. Since Bourgain’s condition is preserved under changes of coordinates onMMandΣ\Sigmaseparately, there ought to be a coordinate-free description of Bourgain’s condition. The AppendixCby Tao provides one such answer, which we summarize below.777Note that a different description of Bourgain’s condition based on the geometry of the characteristic curves ofϕ\phiwas developed in[NAD26].

By Hörmander’s first condition the mapdξ​ϕ:M×Σ→T∗​Σd_{\xi}\phi:M\times\Sigma\to T^{*}\Sigmais a submersion, soM×ΣM\times\Sigmais locally a fiber bundle overT∗​ΣT^{*}\Sigma. The projections of the fibers ofdξ​ϕd_{\xi}\phitoMMare the characteristic curves ofϕ\phi. A choice of local trivializationM×Σ≈T∗​Σ×ℝM\times\Sigma\approx T^{*}\Sigma\times\mathbb{R},(x,ξ)↦(ξ,p,c)(x,\xi)\mapsto(\xi,p,c), amounts to parameterizing each characteristic curve asc↦x​(ξ,p,c)c\mapsto x(\xi,p,c), and a different trivialization reparameterizes the curves. In AppendixC, a special trivialization is chosen. A Legendre-type transformation is used to pass fromT∗​ΣT^{*}\SigmatoT​ΣT\Sigma, which helps motivate the objects in the main characterization below.

## Theorem 1.36.

Ifϕ\phiis a phase function satisfying Bourgain’s condition, then there exist a connection∇∗\nabla^{*}onT​ΣT\Sigmaand two smooth functionsF∗,J∗∈C∞​(T​Σ)F^{*},J^{*}\in C^{\infty}(T\Sigma)such that for allX,Y∈Γ​(T​Σ)X,Y\in\Gamma(T\Sigma), we have
- (1)

Curvature constraint:[∇X∗,∇Y∗]−∇[X,Y]∗+(VX​J∗)​VY−(VY​J∗)​VX=0;\displaystyle[\nabla^{*}_{X},\nabla^{*}_{Y}]-\nabla^{*}_{[X,Y]}+(V_{X}J^{*})V_{Y}-(V_{Y}J^{*})V_{X}=0;(1.81)
- (2)

Compatibility constraint:[∇X∗,VY]−[∇Y∗,VX]=V[X,Y];\displaystyle[\nabla^{*}_{X},V_{Y}]-[\nabla^{*}_{Y},V_{X}]=V_{[X,Y]};(1.82)
- (3)

J∗J^{*}Compatibility constraint:∇X∗VY​J∗=∇Y∗VX​J∗;\displaystyle\nabla^{*}_{X}V_{Y}J^{*}=\nabla^{*}_{Y}V_{X}J^{*};(1.83)
- (4)

Symmetry constraint:∇X∗VY​F∗=∇Y∗VX​F∗,\displaystyle\nabla^{*}_{X}V_{Y}F^{*}=\nabla^{*}_{Y}V_{X}F^{*},(1.84)

whereVVis a vertical lift mapV:Γ​(T​Σ)→Γv​(T​(T​Σ))V:\Gamma(T\Sigma)\to\Gamma_{v}(T(T\Sigma))andVX​VY​F∗V_{X}V_{Y}F^{*}is non-degenerate.

Conversely, if we have∇∗\nabla^{*},F∗F^{*}andJ∗J^{*}, withVX​VY​F∗V_{X}V_{Y}F^{*}non-degenerate, satisfying (1.81), (1.82), (1.83) and (1.84), then we can construct a phase function satisfying Bourgain’s condition.

Theorem1.36gives a convenient way to generate some interesting examples of phase functions satisfying Bourgain’s condition, some of which are recorded in Subsection2.2. Theorem1.36is a step toward systematically classifying the phase functions satisfying Bourgain’s condition, but we do not see how to reduce the constraints much further at this stage.

The following reformulation of Bourgain’s condition in[NAD26]is implicit in the proof of Theorem1.36. In particular, the proof of Theorem1.36essentially takes advantage of the symmetry of∂ξi∂ξj∂ξkϕ\partial_{\xi^{i}}\partial_{\xi^{j}}\partial_{\xi^{k}}\phiafter taking a further derivative of (1.85).

## Proposition 1.37([NAD26]).

Letϕ\phibe a Hörmander-type phase function. Then it satisfies Bourgain’s condition if and only if there exist a smooth scalar-valued functionc:M×Σ→ℝc:M\times\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}and two smooth symmetric matrix-valuedA,B:W×Σ→Symn−1​(ℝ)A,B:W\times\Sigma\to\rm{Sym}_{n-1}(\mathbb{R}), withBBnon-degenerate, such that for all(𝐱,ξ)∈M×Σ({\bf x},\xi)\in M\times\Sigma,∇ξ2ϕ​(𝐱,ξ)=A​(∇ξϕ​(𝐱,ξ),ξ)+c​(𝐱,ξ)​B​(∇ξϕ​(𝐱,ξ),ξ).\displaystyle\nabla_{\xi}^{2}\phi({\bf x},\xi)=A(\nabla_{\xi}\phi({\bf x},\xi),\xi)+c({\bf x},\xi)B(\nabla_{\xi}\phi({\bf x},\xi),\xi).(1.85)

We refer the readers to AppendixCfor the proof of Theorem1.36and further details.

## 1.9Future directions

As the current paper proposes several new concepts, we feel it is natural to propose several future directions to explore as well.


LetΦ\Phibe a map satisfying Hörmander’s condition as in Definition1.13. We define the Kakeya index ofΦ\Phito beinf{dim​(E):E​is aΦ-Kakeya set}.\inf\{\text{dim}(E):E\text{ is a }\text{$\Phi$-Kakeya set}\}.(1.86)

Under the assumption thatΦ\Phiis analytic and semi-algebraic, can we conclude that the Kakeya index ofΦ\Phiis an integer? In particular, when we are in dimension 3, is it the case that the Kakeya index ofΦ\Phiis either22or33? If we take into account Theorem1.34, then an equivalent formulation of the previous question is: IfΦ\Phifails the worst compression condition in Definition1.23, can we conclude thatΦ\Phi-Kakeya sets always have dimension 3?

Roughly speaking, the above question asks whether or not the dimension of aΦ\Phi-Kakeya set must be an integer. However, it is very easy to produce “boring” curved Kakeya sets that have fraction dimensions. For instance, we work in dimension 3, and take Bourgain’s example (1.9). This example admits a two dimensional curved Kakeya set, supported on a hyper-surface. We then make multiple copies of this curved Kakeya set by translating the above hyper-surface, and can produce a curved Kakeya set of an arbitrary fraction dimension between 2 and 3. This also explains the reason of taking inf in (1.86).


More generally, we propose a systematic study ofΦ\Phi-Kakeya maps, according to, for instance, the best possible maximal estimates they satisfy. This work is part of this program, and in particular sets up path geometry as a language to develop a finer classification ofΦ\Phi-Kakeya maps. The work of Bourgain[BOU91]first established that curved Kakeya problems can behave in many different ways. Sogge[SOG99]and Wisewell[WIS03,WIS05]then pioneered their finer study. There has been more recent progress on classifying curved Kakeya problems by a variety of authors[HI22,GWZ24,DGG+24,CGG+25,NAD25,NAD26], but there is much work left.

## 2Examples

## 2.1Hörmander’s condition for curved Kakeya problems

## Example 2.1(Parabolic Kakeya sets).

A Borel setE⊂ℝ2E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}withℒ2​(E)=0\mathcal{L}^{2}(E)=0is called a parabolic Kakeya set if for everyu∈[1,2]u\in[1,2], there existsx∈[−1,1]x\in[-1,1]such that(x,0)+(t,u​t2)∈E,∀t∈[0,1].(x,0)+(t,ut^{2})\in E,\ \forall t\in[0,1].(2.1)

If we setΦ​(x,t;u)=(x+t,(1+u)​t2)\Phi(x,t;u)=(x+t,(1+u)t^{2}), then parabolic Kakeya sets essentially becomeΦ\Phi-Kakeya sets. One can check thatdet​∇(x,t)Φ​(x,t;u)=2​(1+u)​t,{\rm det}\nabla_{(x,t)}\Phi(x,t;u)=2(1+u)t,(2.2)

anddet​[0∇(x,t)Φ∇uΦ∂tΦ∇(x,t)​∂tΦ∇u​∂tΦ]=2​(1+u)​t2.{\rm det}\begin{bmatrix}0&\nabla_{(x,t)}\Phi&\nabla_{u}\Phi\\
\partial_{t}\Phi&\nabla_{(x,t)}\partial_{t}\Phi&\nabla_{u}\partial_{t}\Phi\end{bmatrix}=2(1+u)t^{2}.(2.3)

From these, we see thatΦ\Phidoes satisfy Hörmander’s condition in Definition1.13ift≠0t\neq 0. The degeneracy att=0t=0can be expected because for fixedxxanduu, when we changett, the curve(x+t,(1+u)​t2)(x+t,(1+u)t^{2})always travels in the horizontal direction, and not any other different direction (compared with the traditional Kakeya problem).

If one is interested in studying a parabolic Kakeya maximal function,sup|x|≤1∫01|f​(x+t,u​t2)|​𝑑t,\sup_{|x|\leq 1}\int_{0}^{1}|f(x+t,ut^{2})|dt,(2.4)

then we can apply a standard dyadic decomposition tottwith respect tot=0t=0, and for each dyadic piece, after appropriate scaling we will see that it satisfies Hörmander’s condition in Definition1.13.

## Remark 2.2(Wolff’s parabolic Kakeya sets).

Wolff[WOL97]studied another kind of parabolic Kakeya sets which superficially looks very similar to the one in Example2.1but is fundamentally different.

We say that a Borel setE⊂ℝ2E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}withℒ2​(E)=0\mathcal{L}^{2}(E)=0is called Wolff’s parabolic Kakeya set if for everyu∈[1,2]u\in[1,2]there exists(x,y)∈ℝ2(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}such that(x,y)+(θ,u​θ2)∈E,∀θ∈[0,1].(x,y)+(\theta,u\theta^{2})\in E,\ \forall\theta\in[0,1].(2.5)

It requires Wolff’s deep work[WOL97]to show that Wolff’s parabolic Kakeya sets must have Hausdorff dimension 2. In contrast, it is elementary to show that parabolic Kakeya sets in Example2.1have Hausdorff dimension 2.

## Example 2.3(Nikodym sets).

A Borel setE⊂ℝdE\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}withℒd​(E)=0\mathcal{L}^{d}(E)=0is called a Nikodym set if for everyξ∈𝔹1d−1\xi\in\mathbb{B}^{d-1}_{1}, there existsx∈𝔹1d−1x\in\mathbb{B}^{d-1}_{1}such that𝐋​((x,0);(ξ,1))⊂E,{\bf L}({(x,0);(\xi,1)})\subset E,(2.6)

where𝐋​((x,0);(ξ,1)){\bf L}({(x,0);(\xi,1)})denotes the line segment connecting(x,0)(x,0)and(ξ,1)(\xi,1).

There are several definitions of Nikodym sets in the literature; the one we use here comes from Wisewell[WIS05]. One can find easily that Nikodym sets can “essentially” be realized asΦ\Phi-Kakeya sets for someΦ\Phi. For instance, one can takeΦ​(x,t;ξ)=(x+t​(ξ−x),t).\Phi(x,t;\xi)=(x+t(\xi-x),t).

One can check thatdet​∇(x,t)Φ​(x,t;ξ)=(1−t)d−1,{\rm det}\nabla_{(x,t)}\Phi(x,t;\xi)=(1-t)^{d-1},(2.7)

anddet​[0∇(x,t)Φ∇ξΦ∂tΦ∇(x,t)​∂tΦ∇ξ​∂tΦ]=−1,{\rm det}\begin{bmatrix}0&\nabla_{(x,t)}\Phi&\nabla_{\xi}\Phi\\
\partial_{t}\Phi&\nabla_{(x,t)}\partial_{t}\Phi&\nabla_{\xi}\partial_{t}\Phi\end{bmatrix}=-1,

and thereforeΦ\Phisatisfies Hörmander’s condition in Definition1.13whent≠1t\neq 1.

Let us also remark on what “essentially” refers to. Because of the singularity in (2.7), one would need to do a dyadic decomposition inttwith respect tot=1t=1, similarly to (2.4). Each dyadic piece, after appropriate scaling, can be realized as aΦ\Phi-Kakeya set.


This example is interesting because we see that through the language of general curved Kakeya problems introduced in Subsection1.4, we can “unify” the study of the classical Kakeya problem (for line segments) and the classical Nikodym problem (for line segments). It is well-known in the literature that these two problems are equivalent after pseudo-conformal transformations, which exchange the roles of directions and positions. Using the language of general curved Kakeya problems, we can avoid such transformations.

Fixn≥3n\geq 3and writeξ=(ξ′,ξn−1)\xi=(\xi^{\prime},\xi_{n-1}),𝐱=(x′,xn−1,t){\bf x}=(x^{\prime},x_{n-1},t). The tan-example is given byϕ​(𝐱,ξ)=x′⋅ξ′+12​t2​|ξ′|2+log⁡(sec⁡(t​ξn−1+xn−1)).\displaystyle\phi({\bf x},\xi)=x^{\prime}\cdot\xi^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2}t^{2}|\xi^{\prime}|^{2}+\log(\sec(t\xi_{n-1}+x_{n-1})).(2.11)

Based on the tan-example, we can construct a family of phase functionsϕϵ\phi_{\epsilon}satisfying Bourgain’s condition, none of which satisfies Katz-Wolff condition. But if one takes the limitϵ→0\epsilon\to 0, thenϕϵ\phi_{\epsilon}converges to a Katz-Wolff phase.

## Example 2.5.

Forϵ>0\epsilon>0, we consider a family of phase functionsϕϵ​(𝐱,ξ)=x′⋅ξ′+12​t2​|ξ′|2+1ϵ2​log⁡(sec⁡(ϵ​t​ξn−1+ϵ​xn−1)).\displaystyle\phi_{\epsilon}({\bf x},\xi)=x^{\prime}\cdot\xi^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2}t^{2}|\xi^{\prime}|^{2}+\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\log(\sec(\epsilon t\xi_{n-1}+\epsilon x_{n-1})).(2.15)

whereAϵ=[000ϵ2​vn−12],B=In−1.\displaystyle A_{\epsilon}=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&\epsilon^{2}v_{n-1}^{2}\end{bmatrix},\quad B=I_{n-1}.(2.17)
