Similarly to (2.14),(Xϵ​(w,t;ξ),t)(X_{\epsilon}(w,t;\xi),t)is not direction-equivalent to a family of lines.


Now we takeϵ→0\epsilon\to 0. ThenXϵ​(w,t;ξ)→X0​(w,t;ξ)=(w1−t2​ξ1,w2t−t​ξ2)\displaystyle X_{\epsilon}(w,t;\xi)\to X_{0}(w,t;\xi)=\left(w_{1}-t^{2}\xi_{1},\frac{w_{2}}{t}-t\xi_{2}\right)(2.19)

Fort∼1t\sim 1, we take a diffeomorphismh​(x1,x2,t)=(x1,x2​t,t2)h(x_{1},x_{2},t)=(x_{1},x_{2}t,t^{2}). Then the family of curves(X​(w,t;ξ),t)(X(w,t;\xi),t)is direction-equivalent toh​(X​(w,t;ξ),t)=(w1−t2​ξ1,w2−t2​ξ2,t2),\displaystyle h(X(w,t;\xi),t)=(w_{1}-t^{2}\xi_{1},w_{2}-t^{2}\xi_{2},t^{2}),(2.20)

a family of lines with directions(ξ1,ξ2,1)(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},1).

In the example above, we approximated a phase function satisfying the Katz-Wolff condition with a sequence of phase functions failing the Katz-Wolff condition within Bourgain’s condition. It is therefore tempting to conjecture that the Katz‑Wolff condition generically fails within Bourgain’s condition, i.e., the set of phase functions that violate the Katz‑Wolff condition is open and dense in the space of phase functions satisfying Bourgain’s condition. We expect that a further development along the lines of Theorem1.36will be useful in obtaining this structural information about Bourgain’s condition.

## 2.3Bourgain’s Kakeya-compression example is projectively flat and has extra-worst compression

As mentioned above Definition1.22that Bourgain’s example (1.54) is projectively flat (see (1.55)) and satisfies the extra-worst compression condition. Here we explain more details.

## Example 2.6.

We take Bourgain’s example as in (1.54):Φ​(w,t;ξ):=(w1+t​ξ2+t2​ξ1,w2+t​ξ1,t).\Phi(w,t;\xi):=(w_{1}+t\xi_{2}+t^{2}\xi_{1},w_{2}+t\xi_{1},t).(2.21)

Bourgain’s example admits a two-dimensional Kakeya set. Moreover, it is not difficult to show that inℝ3\mathbb{R}^{3}, the dimension of a curved Kakeya set is always≥2\geq 2. In this sense we say that Bourgain’s example allows certain “worst” behavior. It is perhaps a bit surprising that Bourgain’s example is projectively flat, which looks very much like certain “very nice” property. Indeed, we will show now that Bourgain’s example is worse than we thought, in the sense that every totally geodesic surface gives rise to aΦ\Phi-Kakeya set. This explains why in Definition1.22we introduced the notion “extra-worst” compression condition.


To prove that Bourgain’s example (2.21) has extra-worst compression, it suffices to show that every plane inℝ3\mathbb{R}^{3}gives rise to aΦ~\widetilde{\Phi}-Kakeya set, whereΦ~\widetilde{\Phi}is defined in (1.55). We can always write a planePPin this forma​y1+b​y2+c​y3=d.ay_{1}+by_{2}+cy_{3}=d.(2.22)

IfPPcontainsΦ~​(w,t;ξ)\widetilde{\Phi}(w,t;\xi)for somewwandξ\xi, thena≠0a\neq 0anda​(w1−t​w2+t​ξ2)+b​(w2+t​ξ1)+c​t=d.a(w_{1}-tw_{2}+t\xi_{2})+b(w_{2}+t\xi_{1})+ct=d.(2.23)

Extracting the coefficients ofttimplies{a​w1+b​w2=d,a​(−w2+ξ2)+b​ξ1+c=0.\begin{cases}aw_{1}+bw_{2}=d,\\
a(-w_{2}+\xi_{2})+b\xi_{1}+c=0.\end{cases}(2.24)

We solve this linear system and writewwas a function ofξ\xi{w1=−b2a2​ξ1−ba​ξ2+a​d−b​ca2,w2=ba​ξ1+ξ2+ca.\begin{cases}w_{1}=-\frac{b^{2}}{a^{2}}\xi_{1}-\frac{b}{a}\xi_{2}+\frac{ad-bc}{a^{2}},\\
w_{2}=\frac{b}{a}\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}+\frac{c}{a}.\end{cases}(2.25)

As a result, we see that for any givenξ=(ξ1,ξ2)\xi=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})we can findwwgiven by (2.25), such thatΦ~​(w,t;ξ)∈P,∀t.\widetilde{\Phi}(w,t;\xi)\in P,\quad\forall t.(2.26)

This proves thatPPis aΦ~\widetilde{\Phi}-Kakeya set, and hence Bourgain’s example (2.21) has extra-worst compression.

## 2.4Wisewell’s parabolic Nikodym set is projectively equivalent to the standard Nikodym set

In Wisewell’s PhD thesis[WIS03](see also Wisewell[WIS05]), one of the curved Kakeya/Nikodym problems she studied was the following parabolic Nikodym problem.

## Example 2.7.

LetE⊂ℝ3E\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}be a Borel set withℒ3​(E)=0\mathcal{L}^{3}(E)=0. IfEEsatisfies that for every(w1,w2)(w_{1},w_{2}), there exists(ξ1,ξ2)(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})such that(w1+t​ξ2+t2​ξ1,w2+t​ξ1,t)∈E,(w_{1}+t\xi_{2}+t^{2}\xi_{1},w_{2}+t\xi_{1},t)\in E,(2.27)

for everyt∈[0,1]t\in[0,1], then we say thatEEis a Wisewell’s parabolic Nikodym set.

Note that curves in Wisewell’s parabolic Nikodym set are exactly the same as in Bourgain’s example (2.21). The only difference is that the roles of(w1,w2)(w_{1},w_{2})and(ξ1,ξ2)(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})are exchanged: In Bourgain’s example, we study curved Kakeya sets, which means that we pick(w1,w2)(w_{1},w_{2})depending on(ξ1,ξ2)(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}); in Wisewell’s setting, we pick(ξ1,ξ2)(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})depending on(w1,w2)(w_{1},w_{2}).

Recall from equation2.21that Bourgain’s example admits two dimensional curved Kakeya sets. One question Wisewell asked in[WIS03]was: How about the dimension of the Nikodym sets in Example2.7? The crucial observation that Wisewell made was that Wolff’s hairbrush argument888She indeed applied Katz’s version of the hairbrush argument, see[WIS03].in[WOL95]can be applied in her curved setting, and she concluded that the Nikodym sets in Example2.7have Hausdorff dimension≥5/2\geq 5/2. In other words, for the same family of curves, its associated Kakeya problem may allow certain bad behavior, but its associated Nikodym problem may have certain “best possible” properties. This philosophy played crucial roles in the work[CGG+25]and its applications to the Pierce-Yung problem in[BGH26].


That Wisewell[WIS03]observed that Wolff’s hairbrush argument can be applied in her setting, if translated into our language, means that her Nikodym problem satisfies the Katz-Wolff condition. Now if we think of this fact from the perspectives of the projective geometry of paths, we will very quickly recover Wisewell’s5/25/2-result: Recall from Theorem1.33that the Katz-Wolff condition implies the projective flat condition. In other words, we are able to find a change of coordinates to turn the curves in Example2.7into lines. After realizing this, it is very easy to find the correct change of coordinates, and we just subtract the first entry in (2.27) by the product of the second and third entries. This turns the family of curves in (2.27) into(w1+t​ξ2−t​w2,w2+t​ξ1,t).(w_{1}+t\xi_{2}-tw_{2},w_{2}+t\xi_{1},t).(2.28)

Next, we apply the change of variablesξ2↦ξ2+w2,ξ1↦ξ1,\xi_{2}\mapsto\xi_{2}+w_{2},\ \ \xi_{1}\mapsto\xi_{1},(2.29)

which is allowed for Nikodym problems (but not for Kakeya problems), and we obtain the family of curves(w1+t​ξ2,w2+t​ξ1,t).(w_{1}+t\xi_{2},w_{2}+t\xi_{1},t).(2.30)

This is precisely the classical Nikodym problem.999It is also very helpful to check directly that Example2.7satisfies both Bourgain’s condition and the totally geodesic condition, and therefore also satisfies the Katz-Wolff condition. This was more or less how we realized that Wisewell’s Nikodym problem was equivalent to the classical Nikodym problem.

## 2.5Hilbert’s fourth problem

How Hilbert formulated his fourth problem was quite vague. The formulation we adopt here comes from projective geometry (see for instance[SHE01, Section 12.2]): Characterize all metrics under which all the geodesics locally coincide with Euclidean lines.


There are many important examples that are solutions to Hilbert’s fourth problem, including Hilbert’s metric, Funk’s metric, among others. Here let us pick Funk’s metric to explain the connections to our current discussions.

LetΩ⊂ℝn\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}be a bounded strongly convex domain with smooth boundary∂Ω\partial\Omega. Take two pointsp,q∈Ωp,q\in\Omegawithp≠qp\neq q. We define the distance function (not symmetric)d​(p,q):=ln⁡|z−p||z−q|,d(p,q):=\ln\frac{|z-p|}{|z-q|},(2.31)

wherezzis the intersection point of the rayp+t​(q−p),t≥0,p+t(q-p),\ \ t\geq 0,(2.32)

with the boundary∂Ω\partial\Omega.


This distance function comes from the Funk metric for the domainΩ\Omega. Moreover, all the geodesics under this metric are Euclidean lines. We refer to[SHE01, Section 4.3]for the proof and for more discussions on Funk metrics.


We consider the Carleson-Sjölin operator (see for instance[DGG+24, Section 1.3]) for the Funk metric on the domainΩ\Omega, given by∫Ωei​N​d​(p,q)​f​(q)​a​(p,q)​𝑑q,\int_{\Omega}e^{iNd(p,q)}f(q)a(p,q)dq,(2.33)

whereN∈ℝN\in\mathbb{R},a​(⋅,⋅)a(\cdot,\cdot)is a smooth function supported inΩ×Ω\Omega\times\Omegaand supported away from the diagonalp=qp=q, andd​qdqis given by the Euclidean Lebesgue measure. We would like to view (2.33) as a Hörmander-type operator. To do so, we follow[DGG+24]and consider its reduced Carleson-Sjölin operator. More precisely, letΩ′⊂Ω\Omega^{\prime}\subset\Omegabe a smooth hyper-surface and letΩ0⊂Ω\Omega_{0}\subset\Omegabe a region withd​(Ω′,Ω0)>0.d(\Omega^{\prime},\Omega_{0})>0.(2.34)

Consider∫Ω′ei​N​d​(p,q′)​f​(q′)​a​(p,q′)​𝑑q′,\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}e^{iNd(p,q^{\prime})}f(q^{\prime})a(p,q^{\prime})dq^{\prime},(2.35)

wherea​(p,q′)a(p,q^{\prime})is a smooth function supported onΩ0×Ω′\Omega_{0}\times\Omega^{\prime}, andd​q′dq^{\prime}is given by (for instance) the Euclidean surface measure.


It is not difficult to show (for instance one can repeat the proof in[DGG+24, Section 5.2]) thatd​(p,q′)d(p,q^{\prime})satisfies Hörmander’s condition in Definition1.5, that is, (2.35) is a Hörmander-type operator. Moreover, one can repeat the proof of Lemma 3.2 in[DGG+24]and see that all characteristic curves for the phase functiond​(p,q′)d(p,q^{\prime})are straight. In other words, the curved Kakeya problem associated to the phase functiond​(p,q′)d(p,q^{\prime})is exactly the standard Kakeya problem (for straight lines).

## 3Proof of Proposition1.26

Let us begin by giving a more intuitive explanation for spray spaces.

## Definition 3.1(Conic systems of paths, Shen[SHE01]).

LetMMbe a manifold (M=ℝnM=\mathbb{R}^{n}for us), and letCx⊂Tx​MC_{x}\subset T_{x}Mbe an open cone of the tangent spaceTx​MT_{x}Matx∈Mx\in M.
Let𝒢\mathcal{G}be a collection ofC∞C^{\infty}parametrized curvesσ:(a,b)→M\sigma:(a,b)\to Mwith the following properties:
- (i)

(Existence) For every vectory∈Cx⊂Tx​My\in C_{x}\subset T_{x}Mand everyt∘∈(a,b)t_{\circ}\in(a,b), there is a curveγ:(a,b)→M\gamma:(a,b)\to Min𝒢\mathcal{G}withγ˙​(t∘)=y.\dot{\gamma}(t_{\circ})=y.(3.1)
- (ii)

(Uniqueness) For two arbitrary curvesγ\gammaandσ\sigma, if at somet0∈(a,b)t_{0}\in(a,b)andt1∈(a,b)t_{1}\in(a,b), it holds thatγ˙​(t0)=σ˙​(t1),\dot{\gamma}(t_{0})=\dot{\sigma}(t_{1}),(3.2)

thenγ​(t0+t)=σ​(t1+t),\gamma(t_{0}+t)=\sigma(t_{1}+t),(3.3)

for allt∈(a−t0,b−t0)∩(a−t1,b−t1).t\in(a-t_{0},b-t_{0})\cap(a-t_{1},b-t_{1}).(3.4)
- (iii)

(Invariance) For every curveγ:(a,b)→M\gamma:(a,b)\to Min𝒢\mathcal{G}and everyt∘∈ℝ,λ>0t_{\circ}\in\mathbb{R},\lambda>0, the curveγ~​(t):=γ​(λ​t+t∘),\widetilde{\gamma}(t):=\gamma(\lambda t+t_{\circ}),(3.5)

witha−t∘λ<t<b−t∘λ\frac{a-t_{\circ}}{\lambda}<t<\frac{b-t_{\circ}}{\lambda}(3.6)

is still in𝒢\mathcal{G}.

Definition3.1is a slight modification of the definition of the systems of paths (path spaces) in[SHE01, Section 4.1], in the sense that in Definition3.1we only take a coneCxC_{x}instead of taking the entireTx​MT_{x}M.


There is a one-to-one correspondence between spray spaces and conic systems of paths.

We first start with a spray space in Definition1.25, and construct a conic system of paths. LetU⊂ℝnU\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}be a small open region, and letC⊂ℝnC\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}be an open cone. LetGi:U×C→ℝG^{i}:U\times C\to\mathbb{R}(3.7)

be the smooth function given in Definition1.25. Writex=(x1,x2,…,xn).x=(x^{1},x^{2},\dots,x^{n}).(3.8)

Consider the system of ODEs given byd2​xid​t+2​Gi​(x,d​xd​t)=0,i=1,2,…,n.\frac{d^{2}x^{i}}{dt}+2G^{i}(x,\frac{dx}{dt})=0,\ \ i=1,2,\dots,n.(3.9)

Note that ifx​(t)=(x1​(t),…,xn​(t))x(t)=(x^{1}(t),\dots,x^{n}(t))(3.10)

is a solution to (3.9), then by the homogeneity assumption (1.63), we know thatx~​(t):=x​(λ​t)\widetilde{x}(t):=x(\lambda t)(3.11)

is also a solution to (3.9) for everyλ>0\lambda>0. This, combined with some elementary calculations, shows that the solution curves to (3.9) with an appropriately chosen region of initial data satisfy the requirements of Definition3.1.


Next, we start with a conic system of paths, and try to construct a spray space. This is due to Douglas[DOU27]. Let us briefly sketch the proof here. Our strategy is to use the paths in Definition3.1, write down the ODEs they satisfy and find the functionsGiG^{i}satisfying the homogeneity conditionGi​(x,λ​y)=λ2​Gi​(x,y),∀λ>0.G^{i}(x,\lambda y)=\lambda^{2}G^{i}(x,y),\ \forall\lambda>0.(3.12)

Once theseGiG^{i}are found, we can define spray spaces. More precisely, let𝐆:=(y1,…,yn,−2​G1​(x,y),…,−2​Gn​(x,y)){\bf G}:=(y^{1},\dots,y^{n},-2G^{1}(x,y),\dots,-2G^{n}(x,y))(3.13)

withy=(y1,…,yn),y=(y^{1},\dots,y^{n}),(3.14)

be a vector field onU×CU\times CwhereU⊂ℝnU\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}is a small open region andC⊂ℝnC\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}is an open cone. We often write𝐆=yi​∂∂xi−2​Gi​(x,y)​∂∂yi,{\bf G}=y^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}-2G^{i}(x,y)\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}},(3.15)

where we use Einstein’s summation convention. Note thatx​(t)x(t)is a solution to (3.9) if and only if its liftx^​(t):=(x​(t),d​xd​t​(t))\widehat{x}(t):=\Big(x(t),\frac{dx}{dt}(t)\Big)(3.16)

is an integral curve of𝐆{\bf G}inU×CU\times C. This finishes defining the spray space, and explains the geodesic equation in (1.64) as well.


So far we have finished the discussion on the geometry of spray spaces. At the end of this section, we will prove Proposition1.26. The proof is actually part of the steps in (3.12)–(3.15), and we take this opportunity to write down more details. Let us remark that the proof below is also due to Douglas[DOU27], and it is indeed exactly how Douglas[DOU27]proved that every system of paths induces a spray space.

We start with normal form for the curved Kakeya problem. Consider the map(X​(w,t;ξ),t),(X(w,t;\xi),t),(3.17)

for some mapX:ℝn−1×ℝ×ℝn−1→ℝn−1X:\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\to\mathbb{R}^{n-1}(3.18)

of the formX​(w,t;ξ)=w+t​(ξ+O​(|w|​|ξ|))+t2​O​(|ξ|)+t3​O​(|ξ|)+….X(w,t;\xi)=w+t(\xi+O(|w||\xi|))+t^{2}O(|\xi|)+t^{3}O(|\xi|)+\dots.(3.19)

Here bothO​(|w|​|ξ|)O(|w||\xi|)andO​(|ξ|)O(|\xi|)depend onw,ξw,\xi. It is elementary to see that the collection of curves given by (3.17) forms a conic system of paths. Our goal is to convert the above curves into the form∂t2𝐱=G​(𝐱,𝐩),𝐩:=∂t𝐱,\partial_{t}^{2}\mathbf{x}=G(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p}),\ \mathbf{p}:=\partial_{t}\mathbf{x},(3.20)

where𝐱:ℝn−1×ℝ×ℝn−1→ℝn,{\bf x}:\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\to\mathbb{R}^{n},(3.21)

with appropriate initial data, and the functionGGis homogeneous of degree two in𝐩{\bf p}.


Letα,β∈ℝ\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}be two parameters that will be chosen later. Consider the new parametrization𝐱=(X​(w,α​t+β;ξ),α​t+β).\mathbf{x}=(X(w,\alpha t+\beta;\xi),\alpha t+\beta).(3.22)

We differentiate (3.22) twice, and obtain𝐱=(X​(w,α​t+β;ξ),α​t+β),𝐩=α​((∂tX)​(w,α​t+β;ξ),1),∂t2𝐱=α2​((∂t2X)​(w,α​t+β;ξ),0).\begin{split}&\mathbf{x}=(X(w,\alpha t+\beta;\xi),\alpha t+\beta),\\

Consider the first two equations in (3.23). We would like to expressα,α​t+β,w,ξ\alpha,\alpha t+\beta,w,\xiin terms of𝐱{\bf x}and𝐩{\bf p}, and note that the number of “variables” matches exactly. More precisely, we obtainxn=α​t+β,pn=α;(x1,…,xn−1)=X​(w,xn;ξ),(p1,…,pn−1)=pn​(∂tX)​(w,xn;ξ).\begin{split}&x_{n}=\alpha t+\beta,\ p_{n}=\alpha;\\

This is always solvable because of Hörmander’s condition forXX(from the normal form ofXXwe can also see easily that this has a unique solution). Once we expressα,α​t+β,w,ξ\alpha,\alpha t+\beta,w,\xiusing𝐱{\bf x}and𝐩{\bf p}, we substitute into the last equation in (3.23), and obtain the functionGGas desired in (3.20). ThatGGis homogeneous of degree two in𝐩{\bf p}is immediate. This finishes the proof of the proposition.

## 4Proof of Theorem1.33: The case of phase functions

In this section we will prove Theorem1.33for the case where the curved Kakeya problem arise from a phase function. Item1and Item2do not involve phase functions directly, and we will prove them later forΦ\Phi-Kakeya problems directly (see Section9and Section8). Moreover, for Item4, if a phase functionϕ\phisatisfies both the totally geodesic condition and Bourgain’s condition, then we know (see Theorem1.18) that the inducedΦ\Phi-Kakeya problem also satisfies both the totally geodesic condition and Bourgain’s condition. As a consequence, Item4for the phase functionϕ\phifollows from Item4for the induced mapΦ\Phi, which will be proven in Section6later.

In the rest of this section, we will prove Item3and Item5.

## 4.1Katz-Wolff implying Bourgain

Letϕ\phibe a phase function satisfying Hörmander’s condition. We assume thatϕ\phisatisfies the Katz-Wolff condition, and our goal is to prove that it also satisfies Bourgain’s condition.

Then (4.1) becomes∇y​∂τ2X​(r​z0,0,t2)​(∇yX​(r​z0,0,t2))−1\displaystyle\nabla_{y}\partial_{\tau_{2}}X(rz_{0},0,t_{2})(\nabla_{y}X(rz_{0},0,t_{2}))^{-1}∂τ1X​(r​z0,0,t2)−∂τ1∂τ2X​(r​z0,0,t2)\displaystyle\partial_{\tau_{1}}X(rz_{0},0,t_{2})-\partial_{\tau_{1}}\partial_{\tau_{2}}X(rz_{0},0,t_{2})=λ​(r​z0,t2)​∂τ1X​(r​z0,0,t2).\displaystyle=\lambda(rz_{0},t_{2})\partial_{\tau_{1}}X(rz_{0},0,t_{2}).(4.33)

To make things cleaner, we denote thatW​(r​z0,t2):=the left hand side of (4.1),\displaystyle W(rz_{0},t_{2}):=\text{ the left hand side of \eqref{251208e4-33}},(4.34)Q​(t2):=∇y​∂τ2X​(0,0,t2)​(∇yX​(0,0,t2))−1.\displaystyle Q(t_{2}):=\nabla_{y}\partial_{\tau_{2}}X(0,0,t_{2})(\nabla_{y}X(0,0,t_{2}))^{-1}.(4.35)

Plug all these derivatives ofXXinto (4.1), then we can see that the limit ofλ​(r​z0,t2)\lambda(rz_{0},t_{2})exists, asr→0r\to 0,limr→0|λ​(r​z0,t2)|\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0}|\lambda(rz_{0},t_{2})|=limr→0|W​(r​z0,t2)||∂τ1X​(r​z0,0,t2)|\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{|W(rz_{0},t_{2})|}{|\partial_{\tau_{1}}X(rz_{0},0,t_{2})|}=limr→0|Q​(t2)​r​A​z0||r​A​z0|=|Q​(t2)​A​z0||A​z0|.\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{|Q(t_{2})rAz_{0}|}{|rAz_{0}|}=\frac{|Q(t_{2})Az_{0}|}{|Az_{0}|}.(4.36)

where the last inequality follows from that∂r|W|​|∂τ1X|=|Q​(t2)​A​z0|​|A​z0|​|r|+O​(r2),\displaystyle\partial_{r}|W||\partial_{\tau_{1}}X|=|Q(t_{2})Az_{0}||Az_{0}||r|+O(r^{2}),(4.39)|W|​∂r|∂τ1X|=|Q​(t2)​A​z0|​|A​z0|​|r|+O​(r2).\displaystyle|W|\partial_{r}|\partial_{\tau_{1}}X|=|Q(t_{2})Az_{0}||Az_{0}||r|+O(r^{2}).(4.40)

If we take the limitr→0r\to 0, then both sides of (4.1) vanish. WriteM​(t)=t​A+t2​B+⋯.M(t)=tA+t^{2}B+\cdots.(4.45)

## 4.2Totally geodesic and failing Bourgain’s condition means extra-worst compression

The goal of this subsection to show that if a phase functionϕ\phisatisfies the totally geodesic condition, and fails Bourgain’s condition in Definition1.6, thenϕ\phisatisfies the extra-worst compression condition. We will reduce the problem to the case ofΦ\Phi-Kakeya problems, which is Item5forΦ\Phi-Kakeya problems, and will be proven later in Section7.


Let us be more precise.
The characteristic curves ofϕ\phiare given byΦ​(w,t;ξ)=(X​(w,t;ξ),t)\Phi(w,t;\xi)=(X(w,t;\xi),t)(4.46)

whereX​(w,t;ξ)X(w,t;\xi)satisfies∇ξϕ​(X​(w,t;ξ),t;ξ)=w.\nabla_{\xi}\phi(X(w,t;\xi),t;\xi)=w.(4.47)

Lemma4.2below says that ifϕ\phifails Bourgain’s condition in Definition1.6, thenΦ\Phialso fails Bourgain’s condition in Definition1.17, and therefore the problem is reduced to proving that ifΦ\Phisatisfies the totally geodesic condition and fails Bourgain’s condition in Definition1.17, thenΦ\Phisatisfies the extra-worst compression condition, which is Item5forΦ\Phi-Kakeya problems, and will be proven later in Section7.

## Lemma 4.2.

Letϕ​(x,t;ξ)\phi(x,t;\xi)be a phase function satisfying Hörmander’s condition.
Let us assume thatXXsatisfies Bourgain’s condition in Definition1.17. Thenϕ\phialso satisfies Bourgain’s condition, which is given by Definition1.6.

## Proof of Lemma4.2.

We without loss of generality assume thatϕ\phiis of a normal form at the origin, and our goal is to show thatϕ\phisatisfies Bourgain’s condition at the origin. By definition, thatXXsatisfies Bourgain’s condition in Definition1.17means that∇ξ​∂t2X\nabla_{\xi}\partial_{t}^{2}X(4.48)

is a constant multiple of∇ξ​∂tX.\nabla_{\xi}\partial_{t}X.(4.49)

Let us compute (4.48) and (4.49). By taking derivatives inξ\xion both sides of (4.47), we obtain∇ξ2ϕ​(X,t;ξ)+∇x∇ξ⁡ϕ​(X,t;ξ)​∇ξX=0.\nabla^{2}_{\xi}\phi(X,t;\xi)+\nabla_{x}\nabla_{\xi}\phi(X,t;\xi)\nabla_{\xi}X=0.(4.50)

We differentiate (4.50) intt:∂t∇ξ2ϕ​(X,t;ξ)+∇x∇ξ2⁡ϕ​(X,t;ξ)​∂tX+∂t∇x∇ξ⁡ϕ​(X,t;ξ)​∇ξX+∇x2∇ξ⁡ϕ​(X,t;ξ)​∇ξX​∂tX+∇x∇ξ⁡ϕ​(X,t;ξ)​∂t∇ξX=0.\partial_{t}\nabla^{2}_{\xi}\phi(X,t;\xi)+\nabla_{x}\nabla^{2}_{\xi}\phi(X,t;\xi)\partial_{t}X+\partial_{t}\nabla_{x}\nabla_{\xi}\phi(X,t;\xi)\nabla_{\xi}X+\\
\nabla^{2}_{x}\nabla_{\xi}\phi(X,t;\xi)\nabla_{\xi}X\partial_{t}X+\nabla_{x}\nabla_{\xi}\phi(X,t;\xi)\partial_{t}\nabla_{\xi}X=0.(4.51)

At the origin, we obtain∂t∇ξX=−∂t∇ξ2ϕ.\partial_{t}\nabla_{\xi}X=-\partial_{t}\nabla^{2}_{\xi}\phi.(4.52)

We differentiate (4.51) intt, evaluate at the origin, and only collect the non-zero terms:∂t2∇ξ2ϕ​(X,t;ξ)+∇x∇ξ⁡ϕ​(X,t;ξ)​∂t2∇ξX=0.\partial^{2}_{t}\nabla^{2}_{\xi}\phi(X,t;\xi)+\nabla_{x}\nabla_{\xi}\phi(X,t;\xi)\partial^{2}_{t}\nabla_{\xi}X=0.(4.53)

This, together with (4.52), implies that∂t2∇ξ2ϕ\partial^{2}_{t}\nabla^{2}_{\xi}\phi(4.54)

is a constant multiple of∂t∇ξ2ϕ\partial_{t}\nabla^{2}_{\xi}\phi(4.55)

at the origin, thus finishes the proof thatϕ\phisatisfies Bourgain’s condition at the origin.
∎

## 5Proof of Theorem1.33, Part3: Katz-Wolff implies Bourgain

LetΦ\Phibe a map satisfying Hörmander’s condition. Without loss of generality, we writeΦ​(w,t;ξ)=(X​(w,t;ξ),t).\Phi(w,t;\xi)=(X(w,t;\xi),t).(5.1)

In this section we will show that ifΦ\Phisatisfies the Katz-Wolff condition, then it also satisfies Bourgain’s condition.


We without loss of generality assume thatXXis of a normal form at the origin, that is,X​(w,t;ξ)=w+t​(ξ+O​(|w|​|ξ|))+t2​O​(|ξ|)+t3​O​(|ξ|)+⋯.X(w,t;\xi)=w+t\Big(\xi+O(|w||\xi|)\Big)+t^{2}O(|\xi|)+t^{3}O(|\xi|)+\cdots.(5.2)

In the definition of the Katz-Wolff condition in Definition1.20, we take two curvesΓ1\Gamma_{1}andΓ2\Gamma_{2}, both passing through the origin, one with frequencyξ′=0∈ℝn−1\xi^{\prime}=0\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}and the other with frequencyξ′′=η\xi^{\prime\prime}=\etafor someη≠0\eta\neq 0, i.e.,Γ1={(0,t):|t|≤ϵΦ},Γ2={(X​(0,t;η),t):|t|≤ϵΦ}.\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}=\{(0,t):|t|\leq\epsilon_{\Phi}\},\quad\Gamma_{2}=\{(X(0,t;\eta),t):|t|\leq\epsilon_{\Phi}\}.(5.3)

Katz-Wolff condition says that there existw^=w​(η;t1,t2)\hat{w}=w(\eta;t_{1},t_{2})andξ^=ξ​(η;t1,t2)\hat{\xi}=\xi(\eta;t_{1},t_{2})such that the curveΓ={(X​(w^,t;ξ^),t):|t|≤ϵΦ}\displaystyle\Gamma=\{(X(\hat{w},t;\hat{\xi}),t):|t|\leq\epsilon_{\Phi}\}(5.4)

intersectsΓ1\Gamma_{1}at heightt1t_{1}andΓ2\Gamma_{2}at heightt2t_{2}, respectively, and we know that the set{ξ​(η;t1,t2):t1,t2≠0}\{\xi(\eta;t_{1},t_{2}):t_{1},t_{2}\neq 0\}(5.5)

forms a curve, for all choices ofη≠0\eta\neq 0. In other words, we know thatrank​[∂t1ξ​(η;t1,t2),∂t2ξ​(η;t1,t2)]<2\mathrm{rank}[\partial_{t_{1}}\xi(\eta;t_{1},t_{2}),\partial_{t_{2}}\xi(\eta;t_{1},t_{2})]<2(5.6)

for everyη≠0\eta\neq 0, and everyt1,t2≠0t_{1},t_{2}\neq 0. To simplify future notation, we will write (5.6) asdet(∂t1ξ​(η;t1,t2),∂t2ξ​(η;t1,t2))=0,\det\big(\partial_{t_{1}}\xi(\eta;t_{1},t_{2}),\partial_{t_{2}}\xi(\eta;t_{1},t_{2})\big)=0,(5.7)

where the above determinant can be understood as the column vector whose entries are all the determinants of the2×22\times 2sub-matrices of the(n−1)×2(n-1)\times 2matrix[∂t1ξ​(η;t1,t2),∂t2ξ​(η;t1,t2)][\partial_{t_{1}}\xi(\eta;t_{1},t_{2}),\partial_{t_{2}}\xi(\eta;t_{1},t_{2})]. We begin with two equations,X​(w^,t1;ξ^)=0,\displaystyle X(\hat{w},t_{1};\hat{\xi})=0,\quad\quad\quad\ \(5.8)X​(w^,t2;ξ^)=X​(0,t2;η),\displaystyle X(\hat{w},t_{2};\hat{\xi})=X(0,t_{2};\eta),(5.9)

and repeat an argument similar to that in Section4.1.


We calculate∂t1ξ​(η;t1,t2)\partial_{t_{1}}\xi(\eta;t_{1},t_{2})and∂t2ξ​(η;t1,t2)\partial_{t_{2}}\xi(\eta;t_{1},t_{2}). If we take∂t1\partial_{t_{1}}on (5.8) and (5.9), respectively, then we obtain∇wX​(w^,t1;ξ^)​∂t1w^+∂tX​(w^,t1;ξ^)+∇ξX​(w^,t1;ξ^)​∂t1ξ^=0,\displaystyle\nabla_{w}X(\hat{w},t_{1};\hat{\xi})\partial_{t_{1}}\hat{w}+\partial_{t}X(\hat{w},t_{1};\hat{\xi})+\nabla_{\xi}X(\hat{w},t_{1};\hat{\xi})\partial_{t_{1}}\hat{\xi}=0,(5.10)∇wX​(w^,t2;ξ^)​∂t1w^+∇ξX​(w^,t2;ξ^)​∂t1ξ^=0.\displaystyle\nabla_{w}X(\hat{w},t_{2};\hat{\xi})\partial_{t_{1}}\hat{w}+\nabla_{\xi}X(\hat{w},t_{2};\hat{\xi})\partial_{t_{1}}\hat{\xi}=0.\quad\quad\qquad\qquad\ \(5.11)

Solving (5.10) and (5.11) gives(∇ξX(w^,t1;ξ^)\displaystyle\bigg(\nabla_{\xi}X(\hat{w},t_{1};\hat{\xi})−∇wX(w^,t1;ξ^)∇wX(w^,t2;ξ^)−1∇ξX(w^,t2;ξ^))∂t1ξ^\displaystyle-\nabla_{w}X(\hat{w},t_{1};\hat{\xi})\nabla_{w}X(\hat{w},t_{2};\hat{\xi})^{-1}\nabla_{\xi}X(\hat{w},t_{2};\hat{\xi})\bigg)\partial_{t_{1}}\hat{\xi}=−∂tX​(w^,t1;ξ^).\displaystyle=-\partial_{t}X(\hat{w},t_{1};\hat{\xi}).(5.12)

If we take∂t2\partial_{t_{2}}on (5.8) and (5.9), respectively, then we obtain∇wX​(w^,t1;ξ^)​∂t2w^+∇ξX​(w^,t1;ξ^)​∂t2ξ^=0,\displaystyle\nabla_{w}X(\hat{w},t_{1};\hat{\xi})\partial_{t_{2}}\hat{w}+\nabla_{\xi}X(\hat{w},t_{1};\hat{\xi})\partial_{t_{2}}\hat{\xi}=0,\quad\quad\qquad\qquad\ \(5.13)∇wX​(w^,t2;ξ^)​∂t2w^+∂tX​(w^,t2;ξ^)+∇ξX​(w^,t2;ξ^)​∂t2ξ^=0.\displaystyle\nabla_{w}X(\hat{w},t_{2};\hat{\xi})\partial_{t_{2}}\hat{w}+\partial_{t}X(\hat{w},t_{2};\hat{\xi})+\nabla_{\xi}X(\hat{w},t_{2};\hat{\xi})\partial_{t_{2}}\hat{\xi}=0.(5.14)

Solving (5.13) and (5.14) gives(∇ξX(w^,t1;ξ^)\displaystyle\bigg(\nabla_{\xi}X(\hat{w},t_{1};\hat{\xi})−∇wX(w^,t1;ξ^)∇wX(w^,t2;ξ^)−1∇ξX(w^,t2;ξ^))∂t2ξ^\displaystyle-\nabla_{w}X(\hat{w},t_{1};\hat{\xi})\nabla_{w}X(\hat{w},t_{2};\hat{\xi})^{-1}\nabla_{\xi}X(\hat{w},t_{2};\hat{\xi})\bigg)\partial_{t_{2}}\hat{\xi}=∇wX​(w^,t1;ξ^)​∇wX​(w^,t2;ξ^)−1​∂tX​(w^,t2;ξ^).\displaystyle=\nabla_{w}X(\hat{w},t_{1};\hat{\xi})\nabla_{w}X(\hat{w},t_{2};\hat{\xi})^{-1}\partial_{t}X(\hat{w},t_{2};\hat{\xi}).(5.15)

By the following claim, (5.7) actually saysdet(∂tX​(w^,t1;ξ^),∇wX​(w^,t1;ξ^)​∇wX​(w^,t2;ξ^)−1​∂tX​(w^,t2;ξ^))=0,\det\big(\partial_{t}X(\hat{w},t_{1};\hat{\xi}),\nabla_{w}X(\hat{w},t_{1};\hat{\xi})\nabla_{w}X(\hat{w},t_{2};\hat{\xi})^{-1}\partial_{t}X(\hat{w},t_{2};\hat{\xi})\big)=0,(5.16)

Under the above notation, if we fixt2≠0t_{2}\neq 0, and pickη\etaandt1t_{1}sufficiently close to 0, then we have that∇ξX​(w^,t1;ξ^)−∇wX​(w^,t1;ξ^)​∇wX​(w^,t2;ξ^)−1​∇ξX​(w^,t2;ξ^)\displaystyle\nabla_{\xi}X(\hat{w},t_{1};\hat{\xi})-\nabla_{w}X(\hat{w},t_{1};\hat{\xi})\nabla_{w}X(\hat{w},t_{2};\hat{\xi})^{-1}\nabla_{\xi}X(\hat{w},t_{2};\hat{\xi})(5.17)

is non-singular.

By the normal form ofXX, we see that∇wX​(w,t;ξ)=I+O​(|t|​|ξ|)+O​(|t|2),\displaystyle\nabla_{w}X(w,t;\xi)=I+O(|t||\xi|)+O(|t|^{2}),\(5.18)∇ξX​(w,t;ξ)=t​I+O​(|t|​|w|)+O​(|t|2),\displaystyle\nabla_{\xi}X(w,t;\xi)=tI+O(|t||w|)+O(|t|^{2}),(5.19)

which further implies∇wX​(w,t;ξ)−1​∇ξX​(w,t;ξ)=t​I+O​(|t|​|w|)+O​(|t|2).\displaystyle\nabla_{w}X(w,t;\xi)^{-1}\nabla_{\xi}X(w,t;\xi)=tI+O(|t||w|)+O(|t|^{2}).(5.20)

is non-singular. Note thatw^→0\hat{w}\to 0andξ^→η\hat{\xi}\to\eta, ast1→0t_{1}\to 0. If we sett1t_{1}andη\etasufficiently close to 0, thenw^\hat{w}andξ^\hat{\xi}are sufficiently close to 0. ## 6Proof of Theorem1.33, part5: Totally geodesic and Bourgain imply B.R.-type

In this section we will show that if aΦ\Phi-Kakeya problem satisfies both the totally geodesic condition and Bourgain’s condition, then it is of the Bochner-Riesz-type.


Let us state a quantitative version of our result. LetV:ℝn−1→ℝV:\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\to\mathbb{R}be an analytic function near the origin. If for somen≤p<∞n\leq p<\inftyit holds that‖supω∈𝔹εn−1∫t≃1|​f​(η+t​ω,t−V​(η))​|d​t‖Lp​(𝔹εn−1)≲V,p,ϵ0δ−ϵ0​‖f‖p,\Big\|\sup_{\omega\in\mathbb{B}^{n-1}_{\varepsilon}}\int_{t\simeq 1}|f(\eta+t\omega,t-V(\eta))|dt\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{B}^{n-1}_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim_{V,p,\epsilon_{0}}\delta^{-\epsilon_{0}}\|f\|_{p},(6.1)

Consider theΦ\Phi-Kakeya problem, and we assume thatΦ\Phisatisfies the totally geodesic condition but fails Bourgain’s condition.
By Theorem1.33Part1, we can writeΦ​(w,t;ξ)=(X​(w,t;ξ),t),\Phi(w,t;\xi)=(X(w,t;\xi),t),(7.1)

withX​(w,t;ξ)=w+t​V​(w,ξ).\displaystyle X(w,t;\xi)=w+tV(w,\xi).(7.2)

Parameterize the normal direction of a hyper-plane by(a→,c)(\vec{a},c). We seekw=w​(ξ)w=w(\xi)satisfying(w+t​V​(w,ξ),t)⋅(a→,c)=0.\displaystyle(w+tV(w,\xi),t)\cdot(\vec{a},c)=0.(7.3)

On extracting the coefficients intt,w⋅a→=0\displaystyle w\cdot\vec{a}=0(7.4)V​(w,ξ)⋅a→+c=0\displaystyle V(w,\xi)\cdot\vec{a}+c=0(7.5)

Define the auxiliary functionF​(a→,c,w,ξ)=(V​(w,ξ)⋅a→+c,w⋅a→).\displaystyle F(\vec{a},c,w,\xi)=(V(w,\xi)\cdot\vec{a}+c,w\cdot\vec{a}).(7.6)

We compute∇wF​(a→,0,0,0)=(∇wV​(0)⋅a→,a→).\displaystyle\nabla_{w}F(\vec{a},0,0,0)=(\nabla_{w}V(0)\cdot\vec{a},\vec{a}).(7.7)

We claim that for somea→0\vec{a}_{0}, it holds that111111Here the assumptionn=3n=3occurs.det∇wF​(a→0,0,0,0)≠0.\det\nabla_{w}F(\vec{a}_{0},0,0,0)\neq 0.(7.8)

If this failed for everya→\vec{a}then∂wV​(0)\partial_{w}V(0)is a multiple of the identity, which we already showed is not the case. Thus we can apply the implicit function theorem to findwa→,c​(ξ)w_{\vec{a},c}(\xi)which solvesF​(a→,c,wa→,c​(ξ),ξ)=F​(a→0,0,0,0),\displaystyle F(\vec{a},c,w_{\vec{a},c}(\xi),\xi)=F(\vec{a}_{0},0,0,0),(7.9)

for(a→,c,ξ)(\vec{a},c,\xi)in a small open set around(a→0,0,0)(\vec{a}_{0},0,0).
This means thatwa→,c​(ξ)⋅a→=0,w_{\vec{a},c}(\xi)\cdot\vec{a}=0,(7.10)

andV​(wa→,c​(ξ),ξ)⋅a→+c=V​(0)⋅a→0.V(w_{\vec{a},c}(\xi),\xi)\cdot\vec{a}+c=V(0)\cdot\vec{a}_{0}.(7.11)

Replacingccbyc+V​(0)⋅a→0c+V(0)\cdot\vec{a}_{0}, we see that (7.3) is satisfied forξ\xiin an open set, and for an open set of normal directionsn=(a→,c)n=(\vec{a},c). This finishes the proof of the extra-worst compression phenomenon.

## 8Proof of Theorem1.33Part2: Katz-Wolff implies totally geodesic.

We useΓξ​(𝐱)\Gamma_{\xi}({\bf x})to denote a curve in theΦ\Phi-Kakeya problem passing through𝐱∈ℝn{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}, with directionξ\xi. Take two intersecting curvesℓ0:=Γξ0​(𝐱¯),ℓ1:=Γξ1​(𝐱¯).\ell_{0}:=\Gamma_{\xi_{0}}(\bar{{\bf x}}),\ \ \ell_{1}:=\Gamma_{\xi_{1}}(\bar{{\bf x}}).(8.1)

We generate a surfaceSSby sweeping out a curve inξ1\xi_{1}alongℓ0\ell_{0}:S:=⋃𝐱∈ℓ0Γξ1​(𝐱).S:=\bigcup_{{\bf x}\in\ell_{0}}\Gamma_{\xi_{1}}({\bf x}).(8.2)

ClearlySScontainsℓ0\ell_{0}andℓ1\ell_{1}. ThatSSis totally geodesic (see Definition1.21) follows immediately from the following lemma.

## Claim 8.1.

If a curveℓ2\ell_{2}from theΦ\Phi-Kakeya problem intersectsℓ0\ell_{0}and another point inSS, thenℓ2⊂S\ell_{2}\subset S.Figure 2:Picture of K.W. implies T.G.

## Proof of Claim8.1.

Ifℓ2\ell_{2}hits another point, then it hits one of the “translates”ℓ1′\ell_{1}^{\prime}ofℓ1\ell_{1}. We will use the Katz-Wolff assumption to show every point ofℓ2\ell_{2}is hit by a translateℓ1′′\ell_{1}^{\prime\prime}ofℓ1\ell_{1}.

We apply the Katz-Wolff condition to the two curvesℓ0\ell_{0}andℓ2\ell_{2}: If we useΞ0,2\Xi_{0,2}to denote the collection of all the direction parameters of curves intersecting bothℓ0\ell_{0}andℓ2\ell_{2}, that is,Ξ0,2:={ξ:Γξ​(𝐱)​intersects both​ℓ0​and​ℓ2​for some​𝐱},\Xi_{0,2}:=\{\xi:\Gamma_{\xi}({\bf x})\text{ intersects both }\ell_{0}\text{ and }\ell_{2}\text{ for some }{\bf x}\},(8.3)

then the Katz-Wolff condition says thatΞ0,2\Xi_{0,2}is one-dimensional. Moreover, it is elementary to see that if we fix𝐱0∈ℓ0,𝐱0∉ℓ2{\bf x}_{0}\in\ell_{0},\ {\bf x}_{0}\notin\ell_{2}, then the collection of the direction parametersΞ0,2​(𝐱0):={ξ:Γξ​(𝐱0)​intersects​ℓ2}\Xi_{0,2}({\bf x}_{0}):=\{\xi:\Gamma_{\xi}({\bf x}_{0})\text{ intersects }\ell_{2}\}(8.4)

coincides withΞ0,2\Xi_{0,2}.


Recall thatℓ1′\ell^{\prime}_{1}intersects bothℓ0\ell_{0}andℓ2\ell_{2}, and thereforeξ1∈Ξ0,2.\xi_{1}\in\Xi_{0,2}.(8.5)

This further implies thatξ1∈Ξ0,2​(𝐱0),\xi_{1}\in\Xi_{0,2}({\bf x}_{0}),(8.6)

whenever𝐱0∈ℓ0,𝐱0∉ℓ2{\bf x}_{0}\in\ell_{0},\ {\bf x}_{0}\notin\ell_{2}. In other words, we know thatΓξ1​(𝐱0)\Gamma_{\xi_{1}}({\bf x}_{0})intersectsℓ2\ell_{2}. This finishes the proof thatℓ2⊂S\ell_{2}\subset S.
∎

## 9Proof of Theorem1.33: Part 1: Totally geodesic implies projectively flat

We will prove that a spray space(M,𝒰,𝐆)(M,\mathcal{U},\mathbf{G})satisfying Definition1.32is locally projectively flat. By Theorem1.30, matters reduce to proving that𝐃=0\mathbf{D}=0and𝐖=0\mathbf{W}=0. We prove each of these separately.

## 9.1Showing that𝐃=0\mathbf{D}=0

Fix(x0,y0)∈𝒰(x_{0},y_{0})\in\mathcal{U}. For convenience, pass to a smaller open neighborhoodM′×C⊂𝒰M^{\prime}\times C\subset\mathcal{U}of(x0,y0)(x_{0},y_{0}), whereM′⊂MM^{\prime}\subset Mis open andCCis an open cone. The next lemma lets us straighten the geodesics through a point. This is similar to the geodesic coordinates in Riemannian geometry, except for the technicality thatCCis not necessarilyℝ3\mathbb{R}^{3}.

## Lemma 9.1(Straightening Lemma).

Consider a sprayG:M×C→ℝnG:M\times C\to\mathbb{R}^{n}and fix(x0,y0)∈M×C(x_{0},y_{0})\in M\times C. There exist open neighborhoodsU∋x0U\ni x_{0}andV∋y0V\ni y_{0}, with0∉V0\notin V, a pointp0∉Up_{0}\notin U, and a diffeomorphismF:V→UF:V\to Usuch that
- 1.

F​(y0)=x0F(y_{0})=x_{0}andd​Fy0​(y0)∈ℝ>0​y0dF_{y_{0}}(y_{0})\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}y_{0},
- 2.

FFmaps radial line segments inVVtoGG-geodesic segments. More precisely, for eachyyin a sufficiently small open cone containingy0y_{0}, the curvet↦F​(t​y)\displaystyle t\mapsto F(ty)(9.1)

is a geodesic whenevert​y∈Vty\in V, and it extends to a geodesic passing throughp0p_{0}att=0t=0.

ThusF−1F^{-1}straightens the geodesics throughp0p_{0}into radial line segments.

## Proof of Lemma9.1.

## Lemma 9.3(Linear algebra lemma).

LetTTbe a(1,3)(1,3)-tensor on 3-dimensional vector spaceVVsatisfying the following properties:

- 4.

(Plane property)T​(X,Y,Y)∈Span​(X,Y)T(X,Y,Y)\in\mathrm{Span}(X,Y)for allX,Y∈VX,Y\in V.

ThenT≡0T\equiv 0.

## 10Proof of Theorem1.34

## 10.1A polynomial Wolff axiom

For a mapΦ:ℝ2×ℝ×ℝ2→ℝ3\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}(10.1)

that is analytic and semi-algebraic, we first write it asΦ​(w,t;ξ)=(X​(w,t;ξ),t).\Phi(w,t;\xi)=(X(w,t;\xi),t).(10.2)

Without loss of generality, we assume thatXXis in a normal form.
LetϵΦ>0\epsilon_{\Phi}>0be a sufficiently small constant depending only onΦ\Phi. Let0<κ<ϵΦ0<\kappa<\epsilon_{\Phi}. Take aκ\kappa-net of the direction space𝔹ϵΦ(2)\mathbb{B}^{(2)}_{\epsilon_{\Phi}}, and write it as{ξι}ι\{\xi_{\iota}\}_{\iota}; take aκ\kappa-net of the physical space𝔹ϵΦ(2)\mathbb{B}^{(2)}_{\epsilon_{\Phi}}, and write it as{wι′}ι′\{w_{\iota^{\prime}}\}_{\iota^{\prime}}. We use{(X​(wι′,t;ξι)+κ⋅𝔹ϵΦ(2),t):|t|≤ϵΦ}\{(X(w_{\iota^{\prime}},t;\xi_{\iota})+\kappa\cdot\mathbb{B}^{(2)}_{\epsilon_{\Phi}},t):|t|\leq\epsilon_{\Phi}\}(10.3)

to denote aκ\kappa-tube pointing in the directionξι\xi_{\iota}, and{(X​(wι′,t;ξι),t):|t|≤ϵΦ}\{(X(w_{\iota^{\prime}},t;\xi_{\iota}),t):|t|\leq\epsilon_{\Phi}\}(10.4)

is called the central curve of the tube. We use𝕋ξι\mathbb{T}_{\xi_{\iota}}to denote the collection of allκ\kappa-tubes of the form (10.3) pointing in the directionξι\xi_{\iota}, and𝕋:=∪ι𝕋ξι.\mathbb{T}:=\cup_{\iota}\mathbb{T}_{\xi_{\iota}}.(10.5)

For a constantC≥1C\geq 1and forT∈𝕋T\in\mathbb{T}, we useC​TCTto denote{(X​(wι′,t;ξι)+C​κ⋅𝔹ϵΦ(2),t):|t|≤ϵΦ}.\{(X(w_{\iota^{\prime}},t;\xi_{\iota})+C\kappa\cdot\mathbb{B}^{(2)}_{\epsilon_{\Phi}},t):|t|\leq\epsilon_{\Phi}\}.(10.6)

When applying the polynomial partitioning in[GUT16], we will pick a non-zero polynomialPPof a certain degreeDDdefined onℝ3\mathbb{R}^{3}; without loss of generality we also assume thatPPis a product of non-singular polynomials. LetZ​(P)Z(P)be the zero set of the polynomialPP. DefineW:=𝒩κ​(Z​(P)),W:=\mathcal{N}_{\kappa}(Z(P)),(10.7)

theκ\kappa-neighborhood ofZ​(P)Z(P).

## Definition 10.1.

For a tubeT∈𝕋T\in\mathbb{T}, we say thatTTis tangent toWWifT∩W≠∅,T\cap W\neq\emptyset,(10.8)

and for every non-singular pointz∈Z​(P)z\in Z(P)lying in10​T10T, it holds thatAngle​(vz′​(T),Tz​Z​(P))≤κ1−δΦ,\mathrm{Angle}(v_{z^{\prime}}(T),T_{z}Z(P))\leq\kappa^{1-\delta_{\Phi}},(10.9)

whereTz​Z​(P)T_{z}Z(P)denotes the tangent plane,z′z^{\prime}denotes the point on the central curve ofTTthat is closest tozz, andvz′​(T)v_{z^{\prime}}(T)denotes the tangent direction of the central curve ofTTatz′z^{\prime}. We use𝕋tang\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{tang}}to denote the collection of all tubes tangent toWW.

The main ingredient we need to prove Theorem1.34is as follows.

## Proposition 10.2.

Under the above notation, and under the assumption thatΦ\Phifails the worst compression condition, we have#​{ξι:𝕋tang∩𝕋ξι≠∅}≲Dκ−(2−δΦ),\#\{\xi_{\iota}:\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{tang}}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\xi_{\iota}}\neq\emptyset\}\lesssim_{D}\kappa^{-(2-\delta_{\Phi})},(10.10)

## 10.2Definition of tangency order

Under the assumption that the mapΦ\Phiis analytic and semi-algebraic,

many differentBB’s (we use≈\approxto hide factors of the formκO​(δΦ)\kappa^{O(\delta_{\Phi})}). Finally, we comment that the above analysis is robust: The argument above can be adapted to an analysis in a ball of radiusb>ρb>\rhoinstead of the unit ball, yielding a similar conclusion withbO​(1)b^{O(1)}-loss.

## 10.5Proof of Lemma10.3

We will need the semi-algebraic Łojasiewicz inequality in the proof, which we state below.

## Lemma 10.5(Łojasiewicz inequality ([BCR98, Corollary 2.6.7])).

LetAAbe a closed and bounded semialgebraic set andf,g:A→ℝf,g:A\to\mathbb{R}continuous semialgebraic functions such thatf−1​(0)⊂g−1​(0)f^{-1}(0)\subset g^{-1}(0). Then there exist an integerN>0N>0and a constantc∈ℝc\in\mathbb{R}, such that|g|N≤c​|f||g|^{N}\leq c|f|onAA.

Along with the semi-algebraic Łojasiewicz inequality Lemma10.5, we will need the following volume estimate for semialgebraic fibers. WriteNt​(E)={x∈ℝn:dist​(x,E)<t}N_{t}(E)=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\text{dist}(x,E)<t\}.

## Lemma 10.6(Volume estimate for semialgebraic fibers).

LetPPbe a semialgebraic parameter set and letΓ⊂P×ℝn\Gamma\subset P\times\mathbb{R}^{n}be semialgebraic. WriteΓp:={x:(p,x)∈Γ}\Gamma_{p}:=\{x:(p,x)\in\Gamma\}. Suppose that all the fibersΓp\Gamma_{p}are contained in a fixed compact boxQ⊂ℝnQ\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}and thatdim​Γp≤n−1\text{dim}\Gamma_{p}\leq n-1for allp∈Pp\in P. Then there is a constantCΓ>0C_{\Gamma}>0such that|Nt​(Γp)|≤CΓ​t,for allp∈P,0<t≤1.\displaystyle|N_{t}(\Gamma_{p})|\leq C_{\Gamma}t,\text{ for all $p\in P$, $0<t\leq 1$}.(10.42)

Lemma10.6is a straightforward consequence of the following standard result, which we state in the special case of semi-algebraic sets.

## Lemma 10.7([YC04, Theorem 5.9]).

LetAAbe a semi-algebraic set of dimensionℓ<n\ell<n. Then for any ballBrn⊂ℝnB_{r}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}and for anyη>0\eta>0,|Nη​(A∩Brn)|≤CA​(rℓ​ηn−ℓ+ηn),\displaystyle|N_{\eta}(A\cap B_{r}^{n})|\leq C_{A}(r^{\ell}\eta^{n-\ell}+\eta^{n}),(10.43)

whereCAC_{A}is a constant that depends only on the complexity ofAA141414In[YC04],CAC_{A}depends on thediagramof the semi-algebraic setAA, but the diagram is easily bounded in terms of the complexity.

## Lemma10.7implies Lemma10.6.

The fibersΓp\Gamma_{p}are all semi-algebraic of complexity bounded in terms ofΓ\Gamma, so we can choose a constantCCin Lemma10.7uniform inp∈Pp\in P.
ChooseRRlarge enough thatQ⊂BRQ\subset B_{R}. For a nonempty fiber, putℓp=dim​Γp\ell_{p}=\text{dim}\Gamma_{p}. Applying Lemma10.7toΓp\Gamma_{p}, we get|Nt​(Γp)|\displaystyle|N_{t}(\Gamma_{p})|≤C​(Rℓp​tn−ℓp+tn)\displaystyle\leq C(R^{\ell_{p}}t^{n-\ell_{p}}+t^{n})(10.44)≤C​(max⁡(1,Rn−1)+1)​t\displaystyle\leq C(\max(1,R^{n-1})+1)t(10.45)

forp∈Pp\in Pand0<t≤10<t\leq 1. TakingCΓ=C​(max⁡(1,Rn−1)+1)C_{\Gamma}=C(\max(1,R^{n-1})+1)finishes the proof.
∎

We now have the tools to prove Lemma10.3. We give a brief sketch of the idea first. For fixed(ρ,a,η)(\rho,a,\eta), letTρ,a,η={b:dist​((ρ,a,b),H)<η}T_{\rho,a,\eta}=\{b:\text{dist}((\rho,a,b),H)<\eta\}, The hypothesisdim​Hρ,a<n\text{dim}H_{\rho,a}<nsays thatHρ,aH_{\rho,a}contains nonn-dimensional ball. ## 10.6Proof of Theorem1.34

Now we are ready to prove
Theorem1.34, by applying Proposition10.2and its small “perturbation” (see the end of the proof of Proposition10.2). The argument is almost standard, and we will give a sketch by following the framework of Hickman and Rogers[HR19], Hickman, Rogers and Zhang[HRZ22]and Guth, Hickman and Iliopoulou[GHI19], which further generalize the framework of Guth[GUT16].
It seems to us that the best way to sketch the proof of Theorem1.34is to sketch the proof of Corollary1.35, by following the numerology in[DGG+24]. Once this is done, it will be very clear how the proof of Theorem1.34goes.

Let us only point out the modifications to the numerology in[DGG+24].
We will replaceδΦ\delta_{\Phi}in Proposition10.2byδϕ\delta_{\phi}, a small positive constant depending on the phase functionϕ\phi.
Theorem 4.6 of[DGG+24]is about the polynomial Wolff axiom at the largest scale, and it is now replaced by Proposition10.2. The difference is that in the setting of[DGG+24, Theorem 4.6], the left hand side of (10.10) is essentially bounded byκ−1\kappa^{-1}, which is the best possible scenario one can expect, while in the current setting we only know the boundκ−(2−δϕ)\kappa^{-(2-\delta_{\phi})}for someδϕ>0\delta_{\phi}>0depending onϕ\phi.151515Note thatκ−2\kappa^{-2}is the trivial bound.As a consequence, Corollary 4.8 in[DGG+24]needs to be replaced by‖fι,S2∗‖22≲ϕr2−δϕ′​‖f‖∞2,\left\|f_{\iota,S_{2}}^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim_{\phi}r_{2}^{-\delta^{\prime}_{\phi}}\|f\|_{\infty}^{2},(10.63)

whereδϕ′>0\delta^{\prime}_{\phi}>0is another small constant that depends only onϕ\phi. Note that (10.63) withδϕ′=0\delta^{\prime}_{\phi}=0is trivial.
It is exactly the gainr2−δϕ′r_{2}^{-\delta^{\prime}_{\phi}}over the trivial bound that allows us to obtain the gainκϕ\kappa_{\phi}in Corollary1.35over the universal exponent10/310/3.

Let us be slightly more precise.
In the previous paragraph, we only talked about the polynomial Wolff axiom at the largest scale. The framework of Hickman and Rogers[HR19]and Hickman, Rogers and Zhang[HRZ22]requires that we obtain a non-trivial polynomial Wolff axiom at other scales as well. Imagine that we are discussing polynomial Wolff axioms at the scale1/N21/N_{2}, whereN2≥1N_{2}\geq 1occurred in[DGG+24, page 992]. At this new scale, Proposition 4.9 in[DGG+24]needs to be replaced by‖fι,S2∗‖22≲ϕmin⁡{(Nr2)1δϕ′​r2−δϕ′,(r2−1/2+r2N)}​(r2)δ0​‖f‖∞2,\left\|f_{\iota,S_{2}}^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim_{\phi}\min\left\{\left(\frac{N}{r_{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}_{\phi}}}r_{2}^{-\delta^{\prime}_{\phi}},\left(r_{2}^{-1/2}+\frac{r_{2}}{N}\right)\right\}\left(r_{2}\right)^{\delta_{0}}\|f\|_{\infty}^{2},(10.64)

whereδ0\delta_{0}in[DGG+24, Proposition 4.9]is an extremely small constant that will eventually be sent to zero. On the right hand side of (10.64), there are two terms under the minimum. The first term there is analogous to (10.63), and comes from modifying the proof of Proposition10.2(see the end of the proof of Proposition10.2). The second term under the minimum in (10.64) has nothing to do with the geometry ofϕ\phiand the proof in[DGG+24, Proposition 4.9]works for allϕ\phi.

With (10.63) and (10.64), we can now repeat the entire calculation in[DGG+24], and obtain thatκϕ\kappa_{\phi}in Corollary1.35is given by2​(δϕ′)29+9​δϕ′+6​(δϕ′)2>0.\frac{2(\delta^{\prime}_{\phi})^{2}}{9+9\delta^{\prime}_{\phi}+6(\delta^{\prime}_{\phi})^{2}}>0.(10.65)

This finishes the sketch of the proof of Corollary1.35.

## Appendix AGeometry of paths

We recall some standard material on sprays and their invariants. Our conventions mostly follow[SHE01], though in our setup we work on an open fiberwise conic subset ofT​M∖0TM\setminus 0instead of all ofT​M∖0TM\setminus 0. Since the definitions and results we use work locally onT​M∖0TM\setminus 0, this does not cause any complications. For the more specific material on affine sprays, we also follow[NS94, Chapter 1.3]

## A.1Riemann, Weyl, and Douglas curvatures

The Riemann tensor𝐑\mathbf{R}on a spray space(M,𝒰,𝐆)(M,\mathcal{U},\mathbf{G})is an element ofΓ​(𝒰,End​(π∗​T​M))\Gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathrm{End}(\pi^{*}TM))(A.1)

(some other books might call this the Jacobi endomorphism). What that means is for each(x,y)∈𝒰(x,y)\in\mathcal{U},R(x,y)R_{(x,y)}is a linear map onTx​MT_{x}M, and these maps vary smoothly in(x,y)(x,y).
This is an intrinsic object which can be realized in local coordinates as follows. To do this, we first define theChristoffel symbolsΓj​ki=∂2Gi∂yj​∂yk\displaystyle\Gamma_{jk}^{i}=\frac{\partial^{2}G^{i}}{\partial y^{j}\partial y^{k}}(A.2)

on𝒰\mathcal{U}. Then𝐑(x,y)​v=Rki​(x,y)​vk​∂∂xi|x,\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{(x,y)}v=R_{k}^{i}(x,y)v^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}|_{x},(A.3)

whereRki​(x,y)R_{k}^{i}(x,y)are the local functions on𝒰\mathcal{U}given byRki=(∂Γj​li∂xk−∂Γj​ki∂xl+Γk​si​Γj​ls−Γj​ks​Γl​si)​yj​yl.\displaystyle R_{k}^{i}=(\frac{\partial\Gamma_{jl}^{i}}{\partial x^{k}}-\frac{\partial\Gamma^{i}_{jk}}{\partial x^{l}}+\Gamma_{ks}^{i}\Gamma_{jl}^{s}-\Gamma_{jk}^{s}\Gamma_{ls}^{i})y^{j}y^{l}.(A.4)

It satisfies𝐑(x,y)​(y)=0\mathbf{R}_{(x,y)}(y)=0.


The Weyl tensor𝐖∈Γ​(𝒰,End​(π∗​T​M))\mathbf{W}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathrm{End}(\pi^{*}TM))(A.5)

is another intrinsic object which has a local coordinate realization as follows. DefineAki=Rki−1n−1​Rmm​δki\displaystyle A_{k}^{i}=R_{k}^{i}-\frac{1}{n-1}R_{m}^{m}\delta_{k}^{i}(A.6)

andWki​(x,y)=Aki−1n+1​∂Akm∂ym​yi.\displaystyle W_{k}^{i}(x,y)=A_{k}^{i}-\frac{1}{n+1}\frac{\partial A_{k}^{m}}{\partial y^{m}}y^{i}.(A.7)

Then𝐖(x,y)​(u)=Wki​(y)​uk​∂∂xi|x.\mathbf{W}_{(x,y)}(u)=W_{k}^{i}(y)u^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}|_{x}.(A.8)

We have𝐖(x,y)​(y)=0,trace​(𝐖(x,y))=0\mathbf{W}_{(x,y)}(y)=0,\mathrm{trace}(\mathbf{W}_{(x,y)})=0.
Define thelocal projective sprayassociated with𝐆\mathbf{G}byΠ=yi​∂∂xi−2​Πi​(y)​∂∂yi,\Pi=y^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}-2\Pi^{i}(y)\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}},(A.9)

whereΠi=Gi−1n+1​∂Gm∂ym​yi.\displaystyle\Pi^{i}=G^{i}-\frac{1}{n+1}\frac{\partial G^{m}}{\partial y^{m}}y^{i}.(A.10)

The Douglas curvature𝐃∈Γ​(𝒰,Hom​((π∗​T​M)⊗3,π∗​T​M))\mathbf{D}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathrm{Hom}((\pi^{*}TM)^{\otimes 3},\pi^{*}TM))(A.11)

is given in local coordinates by𝐃(x,y)​(u,v,w)=Dj​k​li​uj​vk​wl​∂∂xi|x\mathbf{D}_{(x,y)}(u,v,w)=D^{i}_{jkl}u^{j}v^{k}w^{l}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}|_{x}(A.12)

whereDj​k​li=∂3Πi∂yj​∂yk​∂yl.\displaystyle D_{jkl}^{i}=\frac{\partial^{3}\Pi^{i}}{\partial y^{j}\partial y^{k}\partial y^{l}}.(A.13)

One has𝐃=0\mathbf{D}=0when𝐆\mathbf{G}is projectively related to an affine spray, and we may further assume the affine spray has symmetric Ricci tensor by passing to the associated local projective spray. As we will see below an affine spray is associated to an affine connection, and affine connections with symmetric Ricci tensor are said to belocally equiaffinein[NS94, Chapter 1.3].
Below we discuss these affine sprays in more detail.

## A.2Special case of affine sprays

TheBerwald tensor𝐁∈Γ​(𝒰,Hom​((π∗​T​M)⊗3,π∗​T​M))\mathbf{B}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{U},\mathrm{Hom}((\pi^{*}TM)^{\otimes 3},\pi^{*}TM))(A.14)

is given in local coordinates by𝐁(x,y)​(u,v,w)=Bj​k​li​uj​vk​wl​∂∂xi|x\mathbf{B}_{(x,y)}(u,v,w)=B_{jkl}^{i}u^{j}v^{k}w^{l}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}|_{x}(A.15)

whereBj​k​li=∂3Gi∂yj​∂yk​∂yl.\displaystyle B^{i}_{jkl}=\frac{\partial^{3}G^{i}}{\partial y^{j}\partial y^{k}\partial y^{l}}.(A.16)

A spray𝐆\mathbf{G}is affine if𝐁=0\mathbf{B}=0. In this case, the Christoffel symbolsΓj​ki​(x,y)=∂2Gi∂yj​∂yk\Gamma_{jk}^{i}(x,y)=\frac{\partial^{2}G^{i}}{\partial y^{j}\partial y^{k}}(A.17)

are independent ofyyandGi​(y)=12​Γj​ki​(x)​yj​yk.\displaystyle G^{i}(y)=\frac{1}{2}\Gamma^{i}_{jk}(x)y^{j}y^{k}.(A.18)

A priori this holds only on𝒰\mathcal{U}, but it of course naturally extends the spray to(M,T​M)(M,TM).
These Christoffel symbols then uniquely define a torsion-free affine connection∇:Γ​(T​M)×Γ​(T​M)→Γ​(T​M)\nabla:\Gamma(TM)\times\Gamma(TM)\to\Gamma(TM)(A.19)

with the same geodesics as the spray𝐆\mathbf{G}. In local coordinates,∇XY=Xj​(∂Yi∂xj+Γj​ki​Yk)​∂∂xi\displaystyle\nabla_{X}Y=X^{j}(\frac{\partial Y^{i}}{\partial x^{j}}+\Gamma_{jk}^{i}Y^{k})\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}(A.20)

In local coordinates, the geodesic equation∇γ˙γ˙=0\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}=0isγ¨i+Γj​ki​γ˙j​γ˙k=0.\displaystyle\ddot{\gamma}^{i}+\Gamma_{jk}^{i}\dot{\gamma}^{j}\dot{\gamma}^{k}=0.(A.21)

One can now view the Riemann curvature of∇\nablaas the(1,3)(1,3)-tensorR∇​(X,Y)​Z=([∇X,∇Y]−∇[X,Y])​Z.\displaystyle R^{\nabla}(X,Y)Z=([\nabla_{X},\nabla_{Y}]-\nabla_{[X,Y]})Z.(A.22)

If𝐑\mathbf{R}is the Riemann curvature of𝐆\mathbf{G}, we have𝐑(x,y)​(X)=Rx∇​(y,X)​y.\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{(x,y)}(X)=R_{x}^{\nabla}(y,X)y.(A.23)

The Riemann curvatureR∇R^{\nabla}satisfies the following properties:
- •

(Skew-symmetry)R∇​(Y,X)=−R∇​(X,Y)R^{\nabla}(Y,X)=-R^{\nabla}(X,Y)
- •

(First Bianchi identity)R∇​(X,Y)​Z+R∇​(Z,X)​Y+R∇​(Y,Z)​X=0R^{\nabla}(X,Y)Z+R^{\nabla}(Z,X)Y+R^{\nabla}(Y,Z)X=0

Similarly the Weyl tensor of∇\nablacan be viewed as the(1,3)(1,3)-tensorW∇​(X,Y)​Z=R∇​(X,Y)​Z−P​(X,Z)​Y+P​(Y,Z)​X,\displaystyle W^{\nabla}(X,Y)Z=R^{\nabla}(X,Y)Z-P(X,Z)Y+P(Y,Z)X,(A.24)

where161616This is the definition of Weyl tensor in[NS94, Page 17], which assumes∇\nablais equiaffine. There is a similar but longer formula for the Weyl tensor when∇\nablais affine but not equiaffine, but this is not necessary.P​(X,Y)=1n−1​trace​(Z↦R∇​(Z,X)​Y).P(X,Y)=\frac{1}{n-1}\mathrm{trace}(Z\mapsto R^{\nabla}(Z,X)Y).(A.25)

That is,W∇W^{\nabla}is the projective trace-free part ofR∇R^{\nabla}. This satisfies skew symmetry and the first Bianchi identity, and in addition the trace-free conditiontrace​(Z↦W∇​(Z,X)​Y)=0.\mathrm{trace}(Z\mapsto W^{\nabla}(Z,X)Y)=0.(A.26)

Similarly to the Riemann tensor, we have𝐖(x,y)​(X)=Wx∇​(y,X)​y.\mathbf{W}_{(x,y)}(X)=W_{x}^{\nabla}(y,X)y.(A.27)

This finishes our discussion on affine sprays.

## Appendix BSchrödinger equation with potentials

Letx∈ℝnx\in\mathbb{R}^{n}andt∈ℝt\in\mathbb{R}. We consider the Schrödinger equation{i​∂tu=−12​Δ​u+V​(x)​u,u​(x,0)=u0​(x),\displaystyle\begin{cases}i\partial_{t}u=-\frac{1}{2}\Delta u+V(x)u,\\
u(x,0)=u_{0}(x),\end{cases}(B.1)

with a potentialV​(x)V(x)satisfying the following growth conditions at infinity:|V​(x)|≤C​(1+|x|2),\displaystyle|V(x)|\leq C(1+|x|^{2}),\qquad(B.2)|∂xαV​(x)|≤Cα,2≤|α|≤Cn.\displaystyle|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}V(x)|\leq C_{\alpha},\quad 2\leq|\alpha|\leq C_{n}.(B.3)

It is well known thatL:u→−12​Δ​u+V​(x)​u,L:u\to-\frac{1}{2}\Delta u+V(x)u,(B.4)

defined onC0∞​(ℝn)C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}), is essentially self-adjoint inL2​(ℝn)L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}), and that the equation has a unique solution given byu​(x,t)=e−i​t​H​u0​(x),\displaystyle u(x,t)=e^{-itH}u_{0}(x),(B.5)

whereHHis the unique self-adjoint extension ofLL. Fujiwara[FUJ80, (14)]proved that the distribution kernelE​(x,t;y)E(x,t;y)ofe−i​t​He^{-itH}has the following structure, at least fort∈(0,δ)t\in(0,\delta)with a sufficiently smallδ\delta.


Let(x​(k,s;y),p​(k,s;y))(x(k,s;y),p(k,s;y))be the solution to the Hamiltonian system corresponding to (B.1),{x˙​(t)=p​(t),p˙​(t)=−∇xV​(x​(t)),t∈(0,δ),x​(0)=y,p​(0)=k.\displaystyle\begin{cases}\dot{x}(t)=p(t),\quad\dot{p}(t)=-\nabla_{x}V(x(t)),&t\in(0,\delta),\\
x(0)=y,\quad p(0)=k.\end{cases}(B.6)

The mappingk↦x​(k,t;y)k\mapsto x(k,t;y)is a global diffeomorphism for everyy∈ℝny\in\mathbb{R}^{n}and everytt. Therefore, for givenxxandtt, we can uniquely selectk=k​(x,t;y)k=k(x,t;y)such that the solution of (B.6) satisfiesx​(k,t;y)=xx(k,t;y)=x. With this solution, we defineS​(x;t,y)=∫0t[12​x˙​(s)2−V​(x​(s))]​d​s.\displaystyle S(x;t,y)=\int_{0}^{t}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}\dot{x}(s)^{2}-V(x(s))\bigg]\text{d}s.(B.7)

The distribution kernel can then be written in the formE​(x,t;y)=1(2​π​i​t)n2​ei​S​(x;t,y)​a​(t,x,y).\displaystyle E(x,t;y)=\frac{1}{(2\pi it)^{\frac{n}{2}}}e^{iS(x;t,y)}a(t,x,y).(B.8)

Our aim is to determine the potentialsV​(x)V(x)for which the phase functionS​(x,t;y)S(x,t;y)satisfies Bourgain’s condition. In[FUJ80, Proposition 1.3], it was shown that∇xS​(x,t;y)=p​(k​(x,t;y),t;y),∇yS​(x,t;y)=−k​(x,t;y).\displaystyle\nabla_{x}S(x,t;y)=p(k(x,t;y),t;y),\quad\nabla_{y}S(x,t;y)=-k(x,t;y).(B.9)

Let us analyze the characteristic curves ofSS. Consider∇yS​(x,t;y)=−w.\displaystyle\nabla_{y}S(x,t;y)=-w.(B.10)

This impliesk​(x,t;y)=wk(x,t;y)=w. Sincek​(x,t;y)k(x,t;y)refers to thekkforcingx​(k,t;y)=xx(k,t;y)=x, we havex​(w,t;y)=xx(w,t;y)=x. Substituting this back into (B.10) yields∇yS​(x​(w,t;y),t;y)=−w.\displaystyle\nabla_{y}S(x(w,t;y),t;y)=-w.(B.11)

Thus, the characteristic curves are parametrized as(x​(w,t;y),t)(x(w,t;y),t).

## Theorem B.1.

Assume thatn≥2n\geq 2and the normalizationV​(0)=∇V​(0)=0V(0)=\nabla V(0)=0.
Letx​(k,t;y)x(k,t;y)solve the equation (B.6). Thenx​(k,t;y)x(k,t;y)satisfies Bourgain’s condition in Definition1.17if and only ifV​(x)=c​|x|2,\displaystyle V(x)=c\,|x|^{2},(B.12)

for some constantcc.

## Proof.

Integrating the differential equation (B.6) with respect tott, we obtain an integral equation:{x​(t)=y+∫0tp​(s)​d​s,p​(t)=k−∫0t∇xV​(x​(s))​d​s.\displaystyle\begin{cases}x(t)=y+\int_{0}^{t}p(s)\,\text{d}s,\\
p(t)=k-\int_{0}^{t}\nabla_{x}V(x(s))\,\text{d}s.\end{cases}(B.13)

To check whetherx​(k,t;y)x(k,t;y)satisfies Bourgain’s condition, we need to examine the rank of the matrix:[∇kx∇yx0∂t∇kx∂t∇yx∇kx∂t2∇kx∂t2∇yxc​∇kx+2​∂t∇kx].\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\nabla_{k}x&\nabla_{y}x&0\\
\partial_{t}\nabla_{k}x&\partial_{t}\nabla_{y}x&\nabla_{k}x\\
\partial_{t}^{2}\nabla_{k}x&\partial_{t}^{2}\nabla_{y}x&c\nabla_{k}x+2\ \partial_{t}\nabla_{k}x\end{bmatrix}.(B.14)

Note that∂t2x​(k,t;y)=−∇xV​(x​(k,t;y))\partial_{t}^{2}x(k,t;y)=-\nabla_{x}V(x(k,t;y)). Taking∇k\nabla_{k}and∇y\nabla_{y}implies∂t2∇kx​(k,t;y)=−∇x2V​(x​(k,t;y))​∇kx​(k,t;y),\displaystyle\partial^{2}_{t}\nabla_{k}x(k,t;y)=-\nabla^{2}_{x}V(x(k,t;y))\nabla_{k}x(k,t;y),(B.15)∂t2∇yx​(k,t;y)=−∇x2V​(x​(k,t;y))​∇yx​(k,t;y),\displaystyle\partial^{2}_{t}\nabla_{y}x(k,t;y)=-\nabla^{2}_{x}V(x(k,t;y))\nabla_{y}x(k,t;y),(B.16)

respectively. After row transformations, the matrix becomes[∇kx∇yx0∂t∇kx∂t∇yx∇kx00c​∇kx+2​∂t∇kx].\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\nabla_{k}x&\nabla_{y}x&0\\
\partial_{t}\nabla_{k}x&\partial_{t}\nabla_{y}x&\nabla_{k}x\\
0&0&c\nabla_{k}x+2\ \partial_{t}\nabla_{k}x\end{bmatrix}.(B.17)

From (B.13), a direct calculation shows the following asymptotic expansion for smalltt:∇kx=O​(t),∇yx=In+O​(t),∂t∇kx=In+O​(t),∂t∇yx=O​(t).\displaystyle\nabla_{k}x=O(t),\ \nabla_{y}x=I_{n}+O(t),\ \partial_{t}\nabla_{k}x=I_{n}+O(t),\ \partial_{t}\nabla_{y}x=O(t).(B.18)

Thus, for smalltt,x​(k,t;y)x(k,t;y)satisfies Hörmander’s condition (1.29), i. e.,[∇kx∇yx∂t∇kx∂t∇yx]\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\nabla_{k}x&\nabla_{y}x\\
\partial_{t}\nabla_{k}x&\partial_{t}\nabla_{y}x\end{bmatrix}(B.19)

is non-degenerate. It then follows from Item3in Theorem1.18thatxxsatisfies Bourgain’s condition if and only if there exists a scalarc=c​(k,t;y)c=c(k,t;y)such that∂t∇kx=c​∇kx.\displaystyle\partial_{t}\nabla_{k}x=c\nabla_{k}x.(B.20)

If the potentialVVhas the formc​|x|2c|x|^{2}, thenx​(k,t;y)x(k,t;y)can be explicitly solved, and a direct calculation confirms that (B.20) is satisfied. Conversely, taking∂t\partial_{t}on both sides of (B.20) yields∂t2∇kx=(∂tc+c2)​∇kx.\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}\nabla_{k}x=(\partial_{t}c+c^{2})\nabla_{k}x.(B.21)

Comparing this result with (B.15), we conclude that∇x2V​(x​(k,t;y))\nabla^{2}_{x}V(x(k,t;y))is a multiple of the identity matrixInI_{n}. Sincekk,yyandttcan be chosen arbitrarily, we see thatVVmust be of formc​|x|2c\,|x|^{2}. This finishes the proof.

## Appendix CAn appendix by Terence Tao: Theorem1.36

## C.1Preliminaries in differential geometry

All manifolds in this section are assumed to be smooth, real, finite-dimensional, and without boundary. All functions, forms, vector fields, etc., will also be assumed to be smooth. IfMMis a smooth manifold,C∞​(M)C^{\infty}(M)will denote the smooth scalar functionsf:M→ℝf:M\to\mathbb{R}onMM, which is a commutative real algebra;T​MTMandT∗​MT^{*}Mdenote the tangent and cotangent bundles, andΓ​(T​M)\Gamma(TM)denotes the space of vector fields ofMM. Algebraically, one can viewΓ​(T​M)\Gamma(TM)as the space ofderivationsX:C∞​(M)→C∞​(M)X\colon C^{\infty}(M)\to C^{\infty}(M), that is to say linear maps that obey the Leibniz ruleX​(f​g)=f​X​(g)+g​X​(f)X(fg)=fX(g)+gX(f)

for allf,g∈C∞​(M)f,g\in C^{\infty}(M). In particularX​1=0X1=0and henceX​c=0Xc=0for any constantc∈ℝc\in\mathbb{R}. The spaceΓ​(T​M)\Gamma(TM)is both aC∞​(M)C^{\infty}(M)-module and a Lie algebra with the usual commutator bracket[X,Y]:=X​Y−Y​X[X,Y]:=XY-YX.


We will be working on manifolds in a local fashion. What this means in practice is that every smooth manifoldMMthat we work with implicitly comes with an origin0M0_{M}, and we reserve the right to restrict the manifoldMMto a sufficiently small open neighbourhood of0M0_{M}whenever we wish; we refer to this operation as “working locally”. Thus, for instance, working locally, everydd-dimensionalMMcan be identified with an open simply connected subset ofℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}, and in particular can be given a set of coordinate functionsx1,…,xd∈C∞​(M)x^{1},\dots,x^{d}\in C^{\infty}(M)and partial derivatives∂x1,…,∂xd∈Γ​(T​M)\partial_{x^{1}},\dots,\partial_{x^{d}}\in\Gamma(TM)obeying the relations[∂xi,∂xj]=0;∂xixj=δij[\partial_{x^{i}},\partial_{x^{j}}]=0;\quad\partial_{x^{i}}x^{j}=\delta_{i}^{j}

fori,j=1,…,di,j=1,\dots,d, whereδ\deltadenotes the Kronecker delta. It is a standard fact that (working locally)Γ​(T​M)\Gamma(TM)is a free module with∂x1,…,∂xd\partial_{x_{1}},\dots,\partial_{x_{d}}as a basis, or in other words everyX∈Γ​(T​M)X\in\Gamma(TM)can be uniquely represented in the formX=Xi​∂xiX=X^{i}\partial_{x^{i}}

for someXi∈C∞​(M)X^{i}\in C^{\infty}(M)fori=1,…,di=1,\dots,d, where we adopt the usual summation conventions. Indeed one can takeXi=X​(xi)X^{i}=X(x^{i})fori=1,…,di=1,\dots,d. At any pointx0∈Mx_{0}\in Mwith coordinatesx0ix^{i}_{0}, and anyf∈C∞​(M)f\in C^{\infty}(M), one has the Taylor expansionf​(x)=f​(x0)+(xi−x0i)​∂xif​(x0)+(xi−x0i)​(xj−x0j)​fi​j​(x)f(x)=f(x_{0})+(x^{i}-x^{i}_{0})\partial_{x^{i}}f(x_{0})+(x^{i}-x^{i}_{0})(x^{j}-x^{j}_{0})f_{ij}(x)

for somefi​j∈C∞​(M)f_{ij}\in C^{\infty}(M), so from several applications of the Leibniz rule one concludes thatX​f​(x0)=Xi​(x0)​∂xif​(x0)Xf(x_{0})=X^{i}(x_{0})\partial_{x^{i}}f(x_{0})

as claimed. In coordinates, the Lie bracket[X,Y][X,Y]of two vector fieldsX=Xi​∂xiX=X^{i}\partial_{x^{i}}andY=Yi​∂xiY=Y^{i}\partial_{x^{i}}can be computed as[X,Y]=(Xj​∂xjYi−Yj​∂xjXi)​∂xi.[X,Y]=(X^{j}\partial_{x^{j}}Y^{i}-Y^{j}\partial_{x^{j}}X^{i})\partial_{x^{i}}.

Afiber bundleis a pair of manifoldsE,ME,Mof dimensionsd+md+m,ddrespectively for somed,m≥0d,m\geq 0, linked by a smooth surjective mapπ:E→M\pi:E\to Mwhich is locally trivial. What this means is that, working locally, one can viewMMas an open subset ofℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}, andEEas an open subset ofℝd+m\mathbb{R}^{d+m}, such thatπ\piis the coordinate mapπ​(x,y):=x\pi(x,y):=x; we refer to such a coordinatization as a(local) trivializationof the bundle.
By abuse of notation, we will sometimes refer to the projection mapπ:E→M\pi:E\to Mas the bundle, withMMreferred to as thebase spaceandEEthetotal space; themm-dimensional spacesπ−1​({x})\pi^{-1}(\{x\})are known asfibers. From the inverse function theorem, we see that a smooth mapπ:E→M\pi:E\to Mlocally gives rise to a fiber bundle if and only if it is a submersion, that is to say its derivative (which in coordinates is and×(d+m)d\times(d+m)matrix) is of full rankdd. Abundle mapT:E→E′T:E\to E^{\prime}between two fiber bundlesπ:E→M\pi:E\to M,π′:E′→M\pi^{\prime}:E^{\prime}\to Mover the same baseMMis a smooth map such thatπ′∘T=π\pi^{\prime}\circ T=\pi, that is to say it maps fibers to fibers.


A fiber bundle induces an embeddingC∞​(M)↩C∞​(E)C^{\infty}(M)\hookleftarrow C^{\infty}(E)that mapsfftof∘πf\circ\pi; by abuse of notation we will view this embedding as an inclusion, thus we will view functions inC∞​(M)C^{\infty}(M)as also being functions onC∞​(E)C^{\infty}(E). A vector fieldZ∈Γ​(T​E)Z\in\Gamma(TE)is said to beverticalif it annihilatesC∞​(M)C^{\infty}(M); the space of such vector fields is aC∞​(M)C^{\infty}(M)-submodule ofΓ​(T​E)\Gamma(TE)and will be denotedΓv​(T​E)\Gamma_{v}(TE). If we introduce local coordinatesxix^{i},i=1,…,di=1,\dots,donMMandxi,yαx^{i},y^{\alpha},i=1,…,di=1,\dots,d,α=1,…,m\alpha=1,\dots,monEEcorresponding to a local trivialization, then an arbitrary vector field inT​ETEtakes the formZ=Xi​∂xi+Yα​∂yαZ=X^{i}\partial_{x^{i}}+Y^{\alpha}\partial_{y^{\alpha}}

for someXi,Yα∈C∞​(E)X^{i},Y^{\alpha}\in C^{\infty}(E)(where Roman indices range over1,…,d1,\dots,dand Greek indices range over1,…,m1,\dots,m), andZZis vertical if and only if theXiX_{i}all vanish.


AnEhresmann connection, orconnectionfor short, on this fiber bundle is aC∞​(M)C^{\infty}(M)-linear map∇:Γ​(T​M)→Γ​(T​E)\nabla\colon\Gamma(TM)\to\Gamma(TE)that maps each vector fieldXXonMMto a vector field∇X\nabla_{X}onEEwhich obeys the compatibility condition∇Xf=X​f\nabla_{X}f=Xf

for allf∈C∞​(M)f\in C^{\infty}(M). In local coordinates, the connection takes the form∇X=Xi​∂xi+Xj​Γjα​∂yα\nabla_{X}=X^{i}\partial_{x^{i}}+X^{j}\Gamma^{\alpha}_{j}\partial_{y^{\alpha}}

for someChristoffel symbolsΓjα∈C∞​(T​E)\Gamma^{\alpha}_{j}\in C^{\infty}(TE), which are given by the formulaΓjα=∇∂xjyα.\Gamma^{\alpha}_{j}=\nabla_{\partial_{x^{j}}}y^{\alpha}.

If∇,∇′\nabla,\nabla^{\prime}are two connections, then∇′−∇\nabla^{\prime}-\nablais aC∞​(M)C^{\infty}(M)-linear map fromΓ​(T​M)\Gamma(TM)toΓv​(T​E)\Gamma_{v}(TE).


A connection∇\nablaisflatif we have the identity[∇X,∇Y]=∇[X,Y][\nabla_{X},\nabla_{Y}]=\nabla_{[X,Y]}(C.1)

for allX,Y∈Γ​(T​M)X,Y\in\Gamma(TM). For instance, every local trivialization gives rise to a flat connection∇X0\nabla^{0}_{X}given by setting all the Christoffel symbolsΓjα\Gamma^{\alpha}_{j}to zero:∇X0=Xi​∂xi.\nabla^{0}_{X}=X^{i}\partial_{x^{i}}.

Conversely, every flat connection (locally) arises from a local trivialization in this fashion; this is a well-known theorem of Frobenius (the flat connection condition is equivalent to the vector fields∇X\nabla_{X}being integrable). In terms of Christoffel symbols, the flatness condition can be expressed as∂xiΓjα−∂xjΓiα+Γiβ​∂yβΓjα−Γjβ​∂yβΓiα=0.\partial_{x^{i}}\Gamma^{\alpha}_{j}-\partial_{x^{j}}\Gamma^{\alpha}_{i}+\Gamma^{\beta}_{i}\partial_{y^{\beta}}\Gamma^{\alpha}_{j}-\Gamma^{\beta}_{j}\partial_{y^{\beta}}\Gamma^{\alpha}_{i}=0.

Two important bundles over a manifoldMMare thetangent bundleT​MTMand thecotangent bundle. In local coordinatesxix^{i}forMM, a point in the tangent bundleT​MTMtakes the form((xi)i=1d,vi​∂xi)((x^{i})_{i=1}^{d},v^{i}\partial_{x^{i}})

for some real numbersxi,vix^{i},v^{i}, and a point in the cotangent bundleT∗​MT^{*}Mtakes the form((xi)i=1d,pi​d​xi)((x^{i})_{i=1}^{d},p_{i}dx^{i})

for some real numbersxi,pix^{i},p_{i}. These bundles project toMMin the obvious fashion. A vector fieldX=Xi​∂xiX=X^{i}\partial_{x^{i}}can be viewed as a mapX:M→T​MX:M\to TMfrom a pointxxto a tangent vectorX​(x)=Xi​(x)​∂xiX(x)=X^{i}(x)\partial_{x^{i}}; this is a bundle map overMM, viewingMMas a trivial bundle over itself. The vector fieldXXcan also be viewed as an elementlXl_{X}ofC∞​(T∗​M)C^{\infty}(T^{*}M)by the formulalX​((x,pi​d​xi))=Xi​(x)​pi.l_{X}((x,p_{i}dx^{i}))=X^{i}(x)p_{i}.

Note that the mapl:X↦lXl:X\mapsto l_{X}is linear overC∞​(M)C^{\infty}(M).


Every scalar functionf∈C∞​(M)f\in C^{\infty}(M)gives rise to adifferentiald​f:M→T∗​Mdf:M\to T^{*}M, defined in local coordinates byd​f​(x)=(x,∂xif​(x)​d​xi),df(x)=(x,\partial_{x^{i}}f(x)dx^{i}),

or via duality aslX​(d​f)=X​fl_{X}(df)=Xf(C.2)

for allX∈Γ​(T​M)X\in\Gamma(TM). This is a bundle map overMM.

## C.2Bourgain’s condition

Now letMMandΣ\Sigmabeddandd−1d-1-dimensional manifolds for somed≥3d\geq 3, and letϕ∈C∞​(M×Σ)\phi\in C^{\infty}(M\times\Sigma)be a “phase function”(x,ξ)↦ϕ​(x,ξ)(x,\xi)\mapsto\phi(x,\xi). For eachx∈Mx\in M, we can viewξ↦ϕ​(x,ξ)\xi\mapsto\phi(x,\xi)as an element ofC∞​(Σ)C^{\infty}(\Sigma); the differentialξ↦dξ​ϕ​(x,ξ)\xi\mapsto d_{\xi}\phi(x,\xi)of this map is then a bundle map fromΣ\SigmatoT∗​ΣT^{*}\SigmaoverΣ\Sigma. We can thus viewdξ​ϕd_{\xi}\phias a bundle map (overΣ\Sigma) fromM×ΣM\times\SigmatoT∗​ΣT^{*}\Sigma. In local coordinates,dξ​ϕ​(x,ξ)=(ξ,∂ξiϕ​(x,ξ)​d​ξi)d_{\xi}\phi(x,\xi)=(\xi,\partial_{\xi^{i}}\phi(x,\xi)d\xi^{i})

(where now Roman indices range in1,…,d−11,\dots,d-1).
We say thatϕ\phiobeys axiom (H1) ifdξ​ϕ:M×Σ→T∗​Σd_{\xi}\phi:M\times\Sigma\to T^{*}\Sigmais a submersion near the origin. In coordinates, this amounts to requiring that thed×(d−1)d\times(d-1)matrix∂xα∂ξiϕ​(x,ξ)\partial_{x^{\alpha}}\partial_{\xi^{i}}\phi(x,\xi)has full rankd−1d-1at the origin (and hence in a sufficiently small neighbourhood). We now assume (H1) henceforth; thusM×ΣM\times\Sigmais now locally a fiber bundle overT∗​ΣT^{*}\Sigma, and thus can be locally trivialized asT∗​Σ×ℝT^{*}\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}. In particular, we now have an identification ofM×ΣM\times\SigmawithT∗​Σ×ℝT^{*}\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}as fiber bundles overΣ\Sigma. The trivial connection∇0\nabla^{0}ofM×ΣM\times\Sigma(viewed as aΣ\Sigma-bundle) then gives rise to a flat connection∇\nablaofT∗​Σ×ℝT^{*}\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}(also viewed as aΣ\Sigma-bundle). The functionϕ\phican now also be viewed as an element ofC∞​(T∗​Σ×ℝ)C^{\infty}(T^{*}\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}). Meanwhile, every vector fieldXXinΓ​(T​Σ)\Gamma(T\Sigma)gives rise to a functionlXl_{X}inC∞​(T∗​Σ)C^{\infty}(T^{*}\Sigma), which by abuse of notation we can also view as an element ofC∞​(T∗​Σ×ℝ)C^{\infty}(T^{*}\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}). From (C.2) and unfolding all the definitions we see that∇Xϕ=lX\nabla_{X}\phi=l_{X}(C.3)

for allX∈Γ​(T​Σ)X\in\Gamma(T\Sigma). From the flatness (C.1) of∇\nabla, we then have thetorsion-free condition∇XlY−∇YlX=l[X,Y]\nabla_{X}l_{Y}-\nabla_{Y}l_{X}=l_{[X,Y]}(C.4)

for allX,Y∈Γ​(T​Σ)X,Y\in\Gamma(T\Sigma). In local coordinates, this is just Clairaut’s theorem∂ξi∂ξjϕ=∂ξj∂ξiϕ.\partial_{\xi^{i}}\partial_{\xi^{j}}\phi=\partial_{\xi^{j}}\partial_{\xi^{i}}\phi.

Conversely, suppose one has a flat connection∇\nablaonT∗​Σ×ℝT^{*}\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}(viewed as aΣ\Sigma-bundle) obeying (C.4) for allX,Y∈Γ​(T​Σ)X,Y\in\Gamma(T\Sigma). Indeed, since∇\nablais flat, it can be locally identified with the trivial connection∇0\nabla^{0}in a trivial bundleℝd×Σ\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Sigma. If we write the identification ofℝd×Σ\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\SigmawithT∗​Σ×ℝT^{*}\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}in local coordinates as(x,ξ)↦((ξ,pi​(x,ξ)​d​ξi),c​(x,ξ))(x,\xi)\mapsto((\xi,p_{i}(x,\xi)d\xi^{i}),c(x,\xi))

for anyF∈C∞​(T∗​Σ×ℝ)F\in C^{\infty}(T^{*}\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}).
The condition (C.4) then becomes the curl-free condition∂ξjpi​(x,ξ)=∂ξipj​(x,ξ).\partial_{\xi^{j}}p_{i}(x,\xi)=\partial_{\xi^{i}}p_{j}(x,\xi).

Since we can locally work in a simply connected domain, we can integrate this condition and writepi​(x,ξ)=∂ξiϕ​(x,ξ)p_{i}(x,\xi)=\partial_{\xi^{i}}\phi(x,\xi)

for someϕ∈C∞​(M×Σ)\phi\in C^{\infty}(M\times\Sigma). In view of this equivalence, we now expect many conditions on the phaseϕ\phito be expressible in terms of conditions on flat connections∇\nablaonT∗​Σ×ℝT^{*}\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}that obey the torsion-free condition (C.4); in particular, we expect that the manifoldMMno longer plays an explicit role. Conversely, suppose that we have a connection∇′\nabla^{\prime}onT∗​ΣT^{*}\Sigma, a functionJ∈C∞​(T∗​Σ)J\in C^{\infty}(T^{*}\Sigma), and aC∞​(Σ)C^{\infty}(\Sigma)-linear mapX↦EXX\mapsto E_{X}fromΓ​(T​Σ)\Gamma(T\Sigma)toΓv​(T​(T∗​Σ))\Gamma_{v}(T(T^{*}\Sigma))which is non-degenerate and obeys the conditions (C.17), (C.18), (C.14), (C.6), (C.8), (C.16). Then, by reversing the above arguments, we see that the connection∇\nabladefined by∇X:=∇X′+c​EX+(EX​J)​∂c,\displaystyle\nabla_{X}:=\nabla^{\prime}_{X}+cE_{X}+(E_{X}J)\partial_{c},(C.19)

is a flat connection obeying (C.4) as well as (H2) and the Bourgain condition withBBgiven by the formulaB​(X,Y)=EX​lY=EY​lX.B(X,Y)=E_{X}l_{Y}=E_{Y}l_{X}.

The fact (C.14) that theEXE_{X}commute with each other allows one to find a coordinate system for the bundleT∗​ΣT^{*}\Sigmain which theEXE_{X}are coordinate vector fields. Indeed, by exponentiating, we get (local) bundle diffeomorphismsexp⁡(EX):T∗​Σ→T∗​Σ\exp(E_{X}):T^{*}\Sigma\to T^{*}\Sigmafor eachX∈Γ​(T​Σ)X\in\Gamma(T\Sigma), with the value ofexp⁡(EX)​(ξ,p)\exp(E_{X})(\xi,p)depending only onXXthrough its valueX​(ξ)X(\xi)atξ\xi. If we arbitrarily pick a smooth sectionω0∈Γ​(T∗​Σ)\omega_{0}\in\Gamma(T^{*}\Sigma)(i.e., a11-form), we can then produce (locally) a bundle diffeomorphismψ:T​Σ→T∗​Σ\psi:T\Sigma\to T^{*}\Sigmaby the formulaψ​((ξ,X​(ξ)))=exp⁡(EX)​(ξ,ω0​(ξ))\psi((\xi,X(\xi)))=\exp(E_{X})(\xi,\omega_{0}(\xi))

for anyξ∈Σ\xi\in\Sigmaand vector fieldX∈Γ​(T​Σ)X\in\Gamma(T\Sigma). Note that every vector fieldX∈Γ​(T​Σ)X\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)canonically induces a vertical vector fieldVX∈Γv​(T​(T​Σ))V_{X}\in\Gamma_{v}(T(T\Sigma)), which can be uniquely defined by the formulaVX​ω=ω​(X)V_{X}\omega=\omega(X)

for all11-formsω∈Γ​(T∗​Σ)\omega\in\Gamma(T^{*}\Sigma), where we identify such one-forms with linear functionals(ξ,v)↦ω​(ξ)​(v)(\xi,v)\mapsto\omega(\xi)(v)onT​ΣT\Sigma; using local coordinates(x,v)(x,v)forT​ΣT\Sigmawe haveVX=Xi​(x)​∂vi.V_{X}=X^{i}(x)\partial_{v^{i}}.

In particular, theVXV_{X}commute with each other:[VX,VY]=0.[V_{X},V_{Y}]=0.(C.20)

By chasing through all the definitions, we see thatVXV_{X}is the pullback ofEXE_{X}byψ\psi:ψ∗​EX=VX\psi^{*}E_{X}=V_{X}. In particular, (C.8) is equivalent toVX​(lY∘ψ)=VY​(lX∘ψ).V_{X}(l_{Y}\circ\psi)=V_{Y}(l_{X}\circ\psi).(C.21)

Using local coordinates(ξ,v)(\xi,v)for the tangent bundleT​ΣT\Sigma, this relation can be written as∂viψj=∂vjψi.\partial_{v^{i}}\psi_{j}=\partial_{v^{j}}\psi_{i}.

We can then locally integrate this asψj=∂vjF∗\displaystyle\psi_{j}=\partial_{v^{j}}F^{*}(C.22)

or in coordinate free notationlX∘ψ=VX​F∗l_{X}\circ\psi=V_{X}F^{*}(C.23)

for allX∈Γ​(T​Σ)X\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)and someF∗∈C∞​(T​Σ)F^{*}\in C^{\infty}(T\Sigma). Asψ\psiis a diffeomorphism,F∗F^{*}must then have non-degenerate vertical Hessian, in that the symmetric bilinear form(X,Y)↦VY​VX​F∗(X,Y)\mapsto V_{Y}V_{X}F^{*}is non-degenerate. (One could similarly integrate the inverse ofψ\psito obtain the Legendre transformFFofF∗F^{*}, as in previous sections, but we will not need to do so here.)


Usingψ\psi, we can pull back the connection∇′\nabla^{\prime}onT∗​ΣT^{*}\Sigmato a connection∇∗\nabla^{*}onT​ΣT\Sigma, and similarly pull back the functionJ∈C∞​(T∗​Σ)J\in C^{\infty}(T^{*}\Sigma)to a functionJ∗∈C∞​(T​Σ)J^{*}\in C^{\infty}(T\Sigma). The relations (C.17), (C.18), (C.16) then pull back to[∇X∗,∇Y∗]−∇[X,Y]∗+((VX​J∗)​VY−(VY​J∗)​VX)=0,[\nabla^{*}_{X},\nabla^{*}_{Y}]-\nabla^{*}_{[X,Y]}+((V_{X}J^{*})V_{Y}-(V_{Y}J^{*})V_{X})=0,(C.24)[∇X∗,VY]=[∇Y∗,VX],[\nabla^{*}_{X},V_{Y}]=[\nabla^{*}_{Y},V_{X}],(C.25)

and∇X∗VY​J∗=∇Y∗VX​J∗.\nabla^{*}_{X}V_{Y}J^{*}=\nabla^{*}_{Y}V_{X}J^{*}.(C.26)

respectively. The relation (C.6) similarly pulls back to∇X∗(lY∘ψ)=∇Y∗(lX∘ψ)\nabla^{*}_{X}(l_{Y}\circ\psi)=\nabla^{*}_{Y}(l_{X}\circ\psi)

and hence by (C.23)∇X∗VY​F∗=∇Y∗VX​F∗.\nabla^{*}_{X}V_{Y}F^{*}=\nabla^{*}_{Y}V_{X}F^{*}.(C.27)

Thus, intriguingly,F∗F^{*}obeys the same equation (C.26) asJ∗J^{*}!


Conversely, suppose one has a connection∇∗\nabla^{*}onT​ΣT\Sigma, and functionsJ∗,F∗∈C∞​(T​Σ)J^{*},F^{*}\in C^{\infty}(T\Sigma)obeying (C.24), (C.25), (C.26), (C.27) withF∗F^{*}(locally) having non-degenerate vertical Hessian. The non-degeneracy then allows us to construct a local bundle diffeomorphismψ:T​Σ→T∗​Σ\psi:T\Sigma\to T^{*}\Sigmausing (C.23), and pushing forward by that diffeomorphism (locally) gives a connection∇′\nabla^{\prime}onT∗​ΣT^{*}\Sigmaand a functionJ∈C∞​(T∗​Σ)J\in C^{\infty}(T^{*}\Sigma). The relations (C.24), (C.25), (C.26), (C.27) then push forward to (C.17), (C.18), (C.16), (C.6) respectively, and (C.20) similarly pushes forward to (C.14). From (C.23), (C.20) we have (C.21), which pushes forward to (C.8). Thus, by previous discussion we have constructed a solution to the Bourgain condition.


To summarize, we have described solutions to the Bourgain condition in terms of just three objects∇∗,J∗,F∗\nabla^{*},J^{*},F^{*}defined on the tangent bundleT​ΣT\Sigma, obeying four relations (C.24), (C.25), (C.26), (C.27) and a non-degeneracy hypothesis.
